Weighted NetKAT DOI: 10.1145/3808318 Journal: PACMPL Volume: 10 PLDI 240 6 pldi26main-p501-p CCS: Theory of computation Verification by model checking CCS: Theory of computation Formal languages and automata theory CCS: Theory of computation Models of computation
Emmanuel Suárez Acevedo, Tiago Ferreira, Kevin Batz, Oliver Bøving, Nate Foster, Alexandra Silva

Abstract
. We introduce weighted NetKAT , a domain-specific language for modeling and verifying quantitative network properties. The language is parametric on a semiring , enabling the treatment of a wide range of quantities in a uniform way. We provide a denotational semantics and an equivalent operational semantics, the latter based on a novel model of weighted NetKAT automata ( 𝖶𝖭𝖪𝖠 \mathsf{WNKA} ) capturing the stateful behavior of our language. With 𝖶𝖭𝖪𝖠 \mathsf{WNKA} , we obtain a class of generic decision procedures for reasoning about quantitative safety and reachability in a fully automatic way, even in the presence of possibly unbounded iteration. We demonstrate the applicability of our framework in a case study using Internet2’s Abilene network as the underlying topology.

A Programming Language For Quantitative Network Verification

1.
Introduction

The field of network verification has emerged as a significant success story for the programming languages community in recent years. The idea is to see the
network as a program
, and model the topology of a network and the configurations of its devices as programs in a domain-specific language, which can then be analyzed to verify properties of interest. This basic approach has been applied successfully at scale in industry, where it has shown to improve the correctness and reliability of networks by catching errors at design time
(
Albab et al. 2022
;
Jayaraman et al. 2019
)
.

Among the numerous network verification frameworks that have been proposed,
NetKAT

(
Anderson et al. 2014
)

stands out for its strong theoretical foundations based on Kleene Algebra with Tests (
KAT
)
(
Kozen 1997
)
.
Indeed, the deep connection between
NetKAT
and finite automata has been instrumental in
facilitating production-grade
(
Moeller
;
authors 2025
)
scalable verification based on automata-theoretic methods
(
Foster et al. 2015
;
Moeller et al. 2024
;
Moeller et al. 2025
)
.

However,
NetKAT
has a critical limitation: its semantics only captures the packet-forwarding behavior of the network. Hence, it can be used to capture basic properties involving the paths that packets take (e.g., reachability, isolation, forwarding loops, etc.). But in many situations, network operators need to reason about richer
quantitative
properties, such as bandwidth, latency, reliability, or security, that cannot be gleaned from topologies and device configurations, but are important for applications such as traffic engineering, fault tolerance, and security.

This paper presents weighted
NetKAT
(
wNetKAT
), a new framework for modeling and reasoning about such quantitative network properties.
wNetKAT
enriches
NetKAT
with new syntactic constructs for assigning and manipulating weights and a semantics that assigns a weight to each execution. At a technical level, we model weights as elements of a semiring—intuitively, semirings arise in networking as their operations model both alternative (e.g., sum) and joint (e.g., product) use of information, across all possible paths in the topology.

Although extending
NetKAT
with weights may appear straightforward initially, there are numerous challenges that arise in the design of the language and in formulating the semantics correctly. For the latter, one has to carefully restrict the semiring to ensure that iteration can be computed and, more interestingly, the operational semantics of the language requires a new automaton model that captures both the presence of weights (very much in line with classical weighted automata) but also correctly accounts for the idiosyncrasies of
NetKAT
semantics. At the level of the expressiveness of the language one has to take into account that the weights needed to compute the relevant quantities might be associated with different parts of the network (e.g. a switch, a link, a port) and therefore the new syntactic constructs need to offer that flexibility.

We provide a thorough formalization of the language and its metatheory including a denotational semantics, language model, operational model using
wNetKAT
automata
, and theorems that equate these different models. The soundness of our automata construction then enables the verification of
wNetKAT
policies at the level of automata. At time of verification the semiring parametricity shines: in different contexts, the nature of the relevant weights varies. For latency we might want to use integers whereas for security we might want to use an ordered set of permission levels. Moreover, the way these quantities need to be combined to propagate through the network to yield the answer to a verification question also varies (e.g., worst-case latency or best-case reliability).

We focus on two classes of quantitative properties in our verification quest:
r
-safety (“Do all paths in the network have weight
at most

r
?”) and
r
-reachability (“Does there exist a path in the network with weight
at least

r
?”). We provide algorithms to decide these verification questions and then illustrate their applicability in a case study. In particular, although
wNetKAT
cannot precisely model the kind of quality of service (QoS) properties that depend on flow-level interactions such as congestion, these verification questions encompass a broad range of network performance characteristics. For example, many quantitative aspects of networks—such as reliability (from historical packet loss) or security (whether a link is trusted)—do not depend on modeling dynamic packet-processing behavior at all. Increased bandwidths and packet-processing rates in networks have also recently enabled network performance to be modeled at a coarser granularity that does not need to directly consider queueing or packet-level congestion
(
Jain et al. 2013
)
.

In summary, this paper makes the following contributions:

•

We develop
wNetKAT
, a semiring-based framework
to facilitate reasoning about quantitative network behaviors such as bandwidth constraints, latency measurements, and reliability metrics.

•

We provide a comprehensive formal treatment of the semantics of
wNetKAT
,
including denotational (
Section

3
),
language-theoretic (
Section

4
), and
operational (
Section

5
) models. The latter is based on a new automaton model—
wNetKAT
automata.
We develop a sound translation, akin to the classical Thompson construction, from
wNetKAT
expressions to
wNetKAT
automata.

•

We establish the exact computation of
wNetKAT
expressions—in particular, providing the first computable semantics for probabilistic extensions of
NetKAT
. This enables algorithms for verifying quantitative network properties:
r
-safety and
r
-reachability (
Section

6
).

•

We showcase the applicability of
wNetKAT
in the setting of Internet2’s
Abilene backbone network, providing worst-/best-case network guarantees over a range of practical network phenomena with automatically generated concrete witnesses and/or counterexamples
(
Section

7
).

We also show that
wNetKAT
subsumes the original semantics of
NetKAT
as well as the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT

(
Foster et al. 2016
;
Smolka et al. 2019b
)
. We include proofs of all formal claims in
the appendix
.

2.
Quantitative Network Verification with
wNetKAT

In this section, we give an overview of the
quantitative network
verification
enabled by
wNetKAT
. First, we briefly recap modeling networks with
NetKAT
, after which we discuss the
challenges of quantitative network behavior.
Finally, we describe the verification of quantitative properties, namely
r
-safety and
r
-reachability, through a computable semantics based on
wNetKAT
automata.

2.1.
Background: Encoding Networks in
wNetKAT

\begin{array}[]{rcl}p&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{sw}=S_{1}\,\mathsf{then}\,p_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\dots\\
l&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{sw}=S_{1}\,\mathsf{then}\,l_{1}\,\mathsf{else}\dots\\
\mathit{in,out}&\triangleq&(\and{\mathsf{sw}=S_{1}}{\mathsf{pt}=1})\,\vee\\
&&\quad(\and{\mathsf{sw}=S_{2}}{\mathsf{pt}=2})\\
\mathit{net}&\triangleq&\mathit{in}\,;(p\,;l\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}\,;\mathit{out}\end{array}

Figure 1
.
Sample network and its encoding in
wNetKAT
.

wNetKAT
is a conservative extension of
NetKAT

(
Anderson et al. 2014
)
, a domain-specific language for modeling a networks’ forwarding policies. When disregarding quantitative aspects, modeling in
wNetKAT
is thus analogous to modeling networks in
NetKAT
. Let us illustrate this by means of an example.

Consider the network in
Figure

1
(left), consisting of two
hosts
and four
switches
. These hosts and switches are connected via links at designated
ports
, giving rise to the network’s topology. Every switch operates according to a forwarding table. For instance, if
S_{1}
receives a packet destined for
H_{2}
, then
S_{1}
will send the packet either via port
3
or port
4
.

In
wNetKAT
, we model networks as intuitive programs, which are called
policies
. More specifically, a policy models how the
fields
of a packet that is being sent through the network are modified over time. In our example, a packet consists of the fields
\mathsf{sw}
(holding the switch the packet is currently at),
\mathsf{pt}
(holding the port the packet is currently at), and
\mathsf{dst}
(holding the packet’s destination). The high-level structure of the policy modeling our example network is depicted in
Figure

1
(right). Consider the top-level policy
\mathit{net}
and let us go over each of its components separately:

\mathit{in}\,;(p\,;l\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}\,;\mathit{out}~.

\mathit{in}
and
\mathit{out}
are predicates specifying the network’s ingress/egress points: a packet can enter/leave the network at port
1
of
S_{1}
or at port
2
of
S_{2}
. The expression
(p\,;l\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}
then models the iterative behavior of the network and is intuitively to be read as follows:
“
p
”
look up in the current switch’s forwarding table at which port the packet is to be placed next, then
“
l
”
send the packet via the corresponding link, then

“
\mathsf{dup}
”
log the current packet’s state in a history, and
“
(\ldots)^{*}
”
repeat.

Both
p
and
l
are basically case distinctions on the current packet’s switch. For instance, if the packet is currently at switch
S_{1}
, then the corresponding sub-policies are given by

(1)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}p_{1}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{dst}=H_{2}\,\mathsf{then}\,(\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 4)\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{dst}=H_{1}\,\mathsf{then}\,\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 1\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{drop}\\
l_{1}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{pt}=3\,\mathsf{then}\,(\mathsf{sw}\leftarrow S_{3}\,;\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 1)\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{pt}=4\,\mathsf{then}\,(\mathsf{sw}\leftarrow S_{4}\,;\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 1)\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{pt}=1\end{array}

p_{1}
branches on the packet’s destination: If the destination is
H_{2}
, then the packet is forwarded via port
3
or
4
, which is modeled via
wNetKAT
’s
choice operator

\oplus
. Similarly,
l_{1}
branches on the current port, and modifies the fields
\mathsf{sw}
and
\mathsf{pt}
according to the network’s topology.

2.2.
Modeling Quantitative Network Behavior in
wNetKAT

Figure 2
.

Sample network with links now weighted by
latency
and
switches weighted by
reliability
, and an optional
retry
link
(dotted line) in the network.

We have just exemplified how to model a network’s packet forwarding behavior in
wNetKAT
when disregarding quantitative aspects. Let us now consider
wNetKAT
’s novel and generic capability of
modeling various quantitative aspects of networks
. Again, we proceed example-driven.

Consider the network in
Figure

2
. The topology and packet-forwarding behavior of this network coincides with the one from
Figure

1
. Both the switches and the links are now annotated with
quantitative information
: the switches are annotated by
success/failure rates
, i.e., the probability of succeeding in forwarding a packet. Links are annotated by
latencies
, i.e., the time it takes for packet to be sent via a particular link.
wNetKAT
enables modeling these aspects in a natural manner.

Probability of each network path

Figure 3
.
Instances of
wNetKAT
: examples of semirings
{(S,\,+,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{0},\,\mathbb{1})}
and their use in networking.

The key idea in modeling this behavior is to introduce a
weighting operation

r\odot p~,

where
p
is a policy and
r
is an element from a fixed
semiring
. Intuitively, this operation says
“execute policy
p
with weight
r
.”

wNetKAT
is parametric in that fixed semiring, rendering it a generic language for modeling all kinds of quantitative aspects.
Figure

3
provides an overview of different semirings and what aspects they are capable of modeling. Details on semirings and their operations are provided in
Section

3
. Let us, for now, gain some intuition for our example network.

With the appropriate semiring (Tropical or Arctic, depending on whether we are interested in best- or worst-case behavior), we can weight policies by
latencies
. To model the latencies attached to the
links
in
Figure

2
, we extend, e.g., the policy
l_{1}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}l_{1}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{pt}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.6211,0.4648}2\mathsf{ms}}\odot(\mathsf{sw}\leftarrow S_{3}\,;\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 1)\ \mathsf{else}\\
&&\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{pt}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.1055,0.6211,0.4648}4\mathsf{ms}}\odot(\mathsf{sw}\leftarrow S_{4}\,;\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 1)\ \mathsf{else}\ {\mathsf{pt}=1}\end{array}

Alternatively, by choosing the Viterbi semiring, we model a
switch’s
forwarding success rate (i.e., its reliability) by weighting the policy encoding its forwarding table. For, e.g.,
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}p_{1}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}98\%}\odot\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{dst}=H_{2}\,\mathsf{then}\,(\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 4)\,\mathsf{else}\dots\\
p_{2}&\triangleq&{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}96\%}\odot\mathsf{if}\,\mathsf{dst}=H_{2}\,\mathsf{then}\,\mathsf{pt}\leftarrow 2\,\mathsf{else}\dots\end{array}

Note how the construct
r\odot p
can be placed in different parts of the network policy to model quantities associated with different components (in the above links and switches). We also emphasize that the choice of the semiring determines the interplay of weighting
\odot
and choice
\oplus
, which influences whether we are modeling best- or worst-case behavior. Recall that switch
S_{1}
may
choose
between forwarding the packet via port
3
or
4
if the destination is
H_{2}
(modeled by
\oplus
in
p_{1}
from (
1
) on page
1
). Regarding, e.g., latencies it is thus natural to distinguish between the best- and the worst-case latency of a packet. Which of these cases we actually model depends on the semiring: The Tropical semiring resolves choices in a latency-minimizing manner (since
\oplus
is interpreted as a minimum) and the Arctic semiring resolves them in a maximizing manner (since
\oplus
maximizes).

2.3.
From Modeling to Verification

Our first goal was to design a language that inherits the modeling aspects from classic
NetKAT
while enabling to model quantitative aspects in a generic and natural way. Our ultimate goal, however, is to obtain
effective procedures to fully automate quantitative reasoning about networks
.

Developing these effective procedures is challenging. In
Section

3
, we will present a semiring-valued denotational semantics

\llbracket p\rrbracket\colon\mathsf{Pk}\to(\mathsf{H}\to\mathcal{S})

where
\mathsf{Pk}
denotes the finite set of packets,
\mathsf{H}
denotes the countably infinite set of histories (think:
“traces”
of packets) and
\mathcal{S}
denotes the chosen semiring. Intuitively,
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi)(h)
is the weight (e.g., worst-case latency)
p
associates with the history
h
on input packet
\pi
. While the denotational semantics provides us with a ground truth for assigning meanings to (weighted) policies, it is not immediately amenable to automation—
wNetKAT
features unbounded iteration, thus denotationally it does not provide a finitary executable description.

Therefore, in the second part of our paper, we develop the novel notion of (finite-state)
wNetKAT
Automata (
WNKA
, for short). The idea is to
compile
—in an algorithmic manner—every
wNetKAT
policy
p
to a
WNKA

\mathcal{A}_{p}
, which accepts a
weighted language over guarded strings
, i.e.,

\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket\colon\mathsf{GS}\to\mathcal{S},\quad\text{where the set of guarded strings is $\mathsf{GS}\cong\mathsf{Pk}\cdot(\mathsf{Pk}\cdot\mathsf{dup})^{*}\cdot\mathsf{Pk}$}~.

Guarded strings represent complete packet traces;
intuitively, we can think of a guarded string
x
as the concatenation of an input packet
\pi
and an output history
h
, i.e., morally
x=\pi\cdot h
, so that

\underbrace{\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(\pi\cdot h)}_{\text{operational semantics enabling effective reasoning}}~{}={}~\qquad\underbrace{\vphantom{\mathcal{A}_{p}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi)(h)}_{\text{denotational semantics}}~.

The
\mathsf{WNKA}

\mathcal{A}_{p}

does
provide us with the finitary description required for the algorithmic verification of quantitative network aspects. To produce these automata models we have devised a Thompson-like construction specific to
wNetKAT
(
Table

1
). Like the classic Thompson construction for regular languages and NFAs, it operates recursively on the structure of a policy. However, as we will see in
Section

5
, this construction is far from being a trivial generalization as we cannot rely in
\varepsilon
-transitions when composing sub-automata (obtained recursively) and we must therefore employ a rather complex on-the-fly epsilon-elimination procedure in a weighted setting. The construction is further complicated by
wNetKAT
’s so-called
carry-on packet semantics
: unlike traditional regular expressions,
NetKAT
is
stateful
, and the output packet of a transition is carried on to the next state. In
wNetKAT
this carry-on packet
additionally
incurs a weight that needs to be accounted for in the Thompson construction. This carry-on packet is also the reason why we cannot directly use classical weighted automata for the operational semantics and need to introduce a new automaton model. Let us now consider the algorithmic problems
\mathsf{WNKA}
s enable us to tackle effectively.

2.4.
Automatic Reasoning in
wNetKAT

Network
wNetKAT

policy
wNetKAT

automaton

Decision
Procedures
encoded
as
compile
(
Theorem

6
)

Figure 4
.
Quantitative verification pipeline of
wNetKAT
instantiated with a semiring
\mathcal{S}
.

Our verification pipeline is depicted in
Figure

4
. With
\mathsf{WNKA}
s, we tackle the following problems:

(1)

{r}
-Safety
: Do
all
—out of possibly infinitely many—traces through the network satisfy a given
upper bound

r
on, e.g., latency or the overall probability of failure?

(2)

r
-Reachability
: Does
there exist
a trace through the network satisfying a given
lower bound

r
on, e.g., a confidentiality measure or the probability of successfully transmitting a packet?

(3)

Computing Weights
: Given an input packet
\pi
and a history
h
, what is the weight
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi)(h)
policy
p
assigns to
h
on
\pi
?

Our corresponding decision procedures are as generic as
wNetKAT
’s modeling capabilities:
In
Section

6
, we provide sufficient conditions on the semiring in order for the above problems to be decidable, and we provide corresponding generic decision procedures based on our
\mathsf{WNKA}
s. The rest of this section is devoted to examples for the above problems.

2.4.1.
Deciding Safety

The verification question of
r
-safety naturally arises when upper-bounding worst-case quantities associated with a network:

Example 0.

We can decide that all traffic in the network from
Figure

2

has a latency of at most
5\mathsf{ms}
. For that, we encode the network in
wNetKAT
(Arctic semiring) and check whether the resulting policy
p
satisfies
5
-safety: For all input packets
\pi
and all histories
h
, we have
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi)(h)\leq 5
.

Moreover, if the safety property is violated, our decision procedure provides
a
witness
: a trace with a weight greater than the safety threshold
r
. For
example, suppose a network provider was considering adding the retry
link
(dotted line) to

Figure

2
. In this case, the network would no longer be
5
-safe
as a packet might be repeatedly forwarded back and forth between switches

S_{3}
and
S_{2}
(accumulating an unbounded latency). Our decision
procedure identifies this updated network as violating the safety property and
provides a witness including the new link.

2.4.2.
Deciding Reachability

The question of
r
-reachability is more natural to ask in settings that model

best-case
analyses, such as modeling reliability with the Viterbi
semiring. In this setting,
r
-reachability corresponds to finding reliable paths
between nodes.

Example 0.

We verify, for the network from
Figure

2
, that host
H_{2}
is reachable from
H_{1}
with a
reliability of at least
90\%
by deciding if the policy

\mathsf{sw}=H_{1}\,;\mathit{net}\,;\mathsf{sw}=H_{2}

is
0.9
-reachable (in the setting of
wNetKAT
instantiated with the
Viterbi semiring).
If the property is satisfied, we provide a sample trace, i.e., an input
packet and output history such that the associated weight is

\geq 0.9
. In this particular instance, the property is satisfied and the
sample trace corresponds to the network path

S_{1}\shortrightarrow S_{3}\shortrightarrow S_{2}
with
a
91.26\%
reliability.

While the network in
Figure

2
is simple, we can reason about much more complicated
networks in
wNetKAT
. In
Section

7
, we model Internet2’s Abilene
backbone network in
wNetKAT
. Abilene features several
nodes
across
major cities in the United States; we use the network to showcase the
verification of quantitative network behavior in a real-world setting.

In the rest of the paper, we make precise the notions covered throughout this
section and then tie everything back to these examples of quantitative
network verification with a case study over the Abilene network. We first provide a formal
definition of the syntax and semantics of
wNetKAT
(
Section

3
),
followed by a language model (
Section

4
) and an
operational semantics based on
wNetKAT
automata
(
Section

5
). Finally, we describe the verification of

r
-safety and
r
-reachability through decision procedures at the level
of
wNetKAT
automata (
Section

6
), and apply them in the real-world setting of
Internet2’s Abilene backbone network (
Section

7
).

A
semiring
is a structure
\mathcal{S}~{}={}~(S,\,+,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{0},\,\mathbb{1})
,
where
S
is a set equipped with two binary operations
+,\cdot\colon S\times S\to S
, and constants
\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1}\in S
satisfying:

(1)

(S,\,+,\,\mathbb{0})
is a commutative monoid,

(2)

(S,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{1})
is a monoid,

(3)

multiplication
distributes over
addition
.

(4)

multiplying with
\mathbb{0}
is
annihilating
.

An
\omega
-continuous semiring
is a structure
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\preceq})
:

(1)

\mathcal{S}
is a semiring,

(2)

(S,\,\mathrel{\preceq})
is an
\omega
-complete partial order,

(3)

\mathrel{\preceq}
is positive, i.e.,
\mathbb{0}
is least element of
\mathrel{\preceq}
,

(4)

both
+
and
\cdot
are
\omega
-continuous,

(5)

\mathcal{S}
admits countable sums, defined as:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}s_{i}~{}={}~\underbrace{\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{i}}_{\mathclap{\text{supremum of partial sums}}}

Figure 5
.
Definition of (
\omega
-continuous) semirings. Semirings offer an
algebraic basis to formalize weights and relevant operations, whereas

\omega
-continuity captures the existence of adequate countable sums,
essential for the semantics of iteration.
All details are provided in

Appendix

A

.

3.
wNetKAT
: Syntax and Semantics

wNetKAT
is parameterized by an
\omega
-continuous semiring
, a class of semirings admitting
countably infinite
sums (see
Figure

5
). We restrict to
\omega
-continuous semirings as unbounded iteration
(-)^{*}
is naturally captured by an infinite choice—the semantics of which then needs to make use of these countable sums to be well-defined. We discuss this further in
Section

3.2
.

We fix an
\omega
-continuous semiring
\mathcal{S}
throughout this section and the rest of the paper. To emphasize the instantiation with a given semiring
\mathcal{S}
, we sometimes refer to
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
(and write just
wNetKAT
when the semiring is clear from context).

3.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
wNetKAT
is shown in
Figure

6
(left).
We fix a
finite
set of

(packet header) values

\mathsf{Val}
that each of the finitely many

fields

\mathsf{F}
can take. A
packet

\pi
in the set

\mathsf{Pk}
is thus a finite function of type
\mathsf{F}\to\mathsf{Val}
,
assigning a value to each field. We usually write
\pi.f

instead of
\pi(f)
and denote packets in record notation

\{f_{1}=n_{1},\dots,f_{k}=n_{k}\}
. A
(packet) history

h=\pi{::}\hbar
is a
non-empty
list of packets, where

\pi
is the
head packet
and
\hbar
is the (possibly empty)
tail
.
We sometimes abuse notation, and write
\pi{::}h

instead of
\pi{::}\hbar
.
Finally, elements
r
of the semiring
\mathcal{S}
are called
weights
.

Predicates

t
are Boolean combinations of
\mathsf{false}
,
\mathsf{true}
, and
tests
of the form
f=n
. They act as filters: If the current packet does not satisfy
t
, the packet is dropped.
The
modification

f\leftarrow n
assigns the value
n
to the field
f
in the current packet.
Sequential composition

p\,;q
first executes
p
and then executes
q
.
Weighting

r\odot p
weights the execution of
p
by the semiring element
r
. The
choice

p\oplus q
executes either
p
or
q
. The primitive
\mathsf{dup}
can be understood as a logging command for keeping track of a packet’s trajectory through a network.
Iteration

p^{*}
is, intuitively, a countably infinite choice between terminating or keeping iterating
p
, i.e., schematically,
p^{*}
is equivalent to

\mathsf{skip}\quad\oplus\quad p\quad\oplus\quad p\,;p\quad\oplus\quad p\,;p\,;p\quad\oplus\quad\ldots~,

where we write
\mathsf{skip}
(
\mathsf{drop}
) instead of
\mathsf{true}
(
\mathsf{false}
) to emphasize the behavior of the predicate when used as a policy. Finally, we can also encode usual control-flow structures in
wNetKAT
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{skip}&\triangleq&\mathsf{true}\\
\mathsf{drop}&\triangleq&\mathsf{false}\\
\end{array}\qquad\qquad\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{if}\,t\,\mathsf{then}\,p\,\mathsf{else}\,q&\triangleq&t\,;p\oplus\neg t\,;q\\
\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p&\triangleq&(t\,;p)^{*}\,;\neg t\end{array}

These encodings are justified by the semantics of
wNetKAT
, which we go over next.

3.2.
Semantics

Syntax

\begin{array}[]{r@{\ \ }r@{~}c@{~}l@{\ \ }l}\textrm{Values}&\mathsf{Val}\ni n&::=\mathrlap{v_{1}\mid\ldots\mid v_{n}}&\\
\textrm{Fields}&\mathsf{F}\ni f&::=&\mathrlap{f_{1}\mid\ldots\mid f_{k}}\\
\textrm{Packets}&\mathsf{Pk}\ni\pi,\alpha,\beta,\gamma&::=&\mathrlap{\{f_{1}=n_{1},\dots,f_{k}=n_{k}\}}\\
\textrm{Histories}&\mathsf{H}\ni h&::=&\mathrlap{\pi{::}\hbar}\\
&\hbar&::=&\mathrlap{\langle\rangle\mid\pi{::}\hbar}\\
\textrm{Weights}&\mathcal{S}\ni r,s\\
\textrm{Predicates}&\mathsf{Pred}\ni t,u&::=&\mathsf{false}&\textit{False/Drop}\\
&&\mid&\mathsf{true}&\textit{True/Skip}\\
&&\mid&f=n&\textit{Test}\\
&&\mid&t\,\vee\,u&\textit{Disjunction}\\
&&\mid&\and{t}{u}&\textit{Conjunction}\\
&&\mid&\neg t&\textit{Negation}\\
\textrm{Policies}&\mathsf{Pol}\ni p,q&\ ::=&t&\textit{Filter}\\
&&\mid&f\leftarrow n&\textit{Modification}\\
&&\mid&\mathsf{dup}&\textit{Duplication}\\
&&\mid&p\,;q&\textit{Seq.\ Comp.}\\
&&\mid&r\odot p&\textit{Weighting}\\
&&\mid&p\oplus q&\textit{Choice}\\
&&\mid&p^{*}&\textit{Iteration}\\
\end{array}

Semantics

\begin{array}[]{r@{~~}c@{~~}l}\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}&\colon&\mathsf{Pk}\to 2\\
\llbracket\mathsf{false}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&{}=&0\\
\llbracket\mathsf{true}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&{}=&1\\
\llbracket f=n\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&{}=&\left[\pi.f=n\right]\\
\llbracket t\,\vee\,u\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&{}=&\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)\vee\llbracket u\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)\\
\llbracket\and{t}{u}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&=&\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)\wedge\llbracket u\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)\\
\llbracket\neg t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)&{}=&\left[\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)=0\right]\\
\llbracket p\rrbracket&\colon&{\mathsf{H}}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})\\
\llbracket t\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)&{}=&\left[\llbracket t\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}(\pi)=1\right]\cdot\eta(\pi{::}\hbar)\\
\llbracket f\leftarrow n\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)&{}=&\eta\left(\pi[f\coloneq n]{::}\hbar\right)\\
\llbracket\mathsf{dup}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)&{}=&\eta(\pi{::}\pi{::}\hbar)\\
\llbracket p\,;q\rrbracket(h)&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket\\
\llbracket r\odot p\rrbracket(h)&{}=&r\cdot\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\\
\llbracket p\oplus q\rrbracket(h)&{}=&\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)+\llbracket q\rrbracket(h)\\
\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket(h)&{}=&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(n)}\rrbracket(h)\\
\lx@intercol\text{where }~$${p}^{(0)}$ = $\mathsf{skip}$$~\text{ and }~$${p}^{(n+1)}$ = $p\,;{p}^{(n)}$$\hfil\lx@intercol\end{array}

Figure 6
.
Syntax and Semantics of
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
, where
\mathcal{S}=(S,\,+,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{0},\,\mathbb{1})
is an
\omega
-continuous semiring. We assume that the operators bind stronger in the order:
\neg{}
,
\and{}{}
,
\,\vee\,
,
\,;
,
\odot
,
\oplus
.

The semantics of
wNetKAT
is shown in
Figure

6
(right). In what follows, we first introduce so-called
weightings
—the central semantic objects. We then detail the semantics of the individual constructs and state various desirable properties such as a fixed point characterization of iteration.

3.2.1.
Weightings

Intuitively, a policy
p
takes as input a history
h
and produces a set of output histories
h^{\prime}
, where
each output history is weighted by some element from
\mathcal{S}
. To formalize an appropriate semantic domain, we introduce the following central objects:

Definition 0 (Weightings).

We define
weightings over a set
X
as the tuple

(\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X),\,\eta,\,\gg\!=)
,
where:

(1)

\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\triangleq\{m\colon X\rightarrow\mathcal{S}~|~\mathsf{supp}(m)~\text{is countable}\}
with
\mathsf{supp}(m)\triangleq\{x\in X~|~m(x)\neq\mathbb{0}\}
,

(2)

\eta\colon X\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
is the
unit
, defined as
\eta(x)\triangleq\lambda y.\,[x=y]
.
1
1

1

Here, and throughout the paper, we use Iverson bracket notation:
[P]=\mathbb{1}
if the proposition
P
holds and
[P]=\mathbb{0}
otherwise.

(3)

\gg\!=\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)\to(X\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X))\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
is the
bind
, defined as (using infix notation)

m\gg\!=f\quad{}\triangleq{}\quad\lambda y.\,{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}\end{subarray}}m(x)\cdot f(x)(y)}

This sum is well-defined as
\mathsf{supp}(m)
is countable and
\omega
-continuous semirings admit countable sums.

We often call weightings the elements of
\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
and denote them by
m,m^{\prime}
, and variations thereof.
\mathsf{supp}(m)
is called the
support
of
m
.
It is easy to check that the monad axioms
hold for the operations as defined above, and therefore
(\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X),\,\eta,\,\gg\!=)
forms a monad.

We lift the operations and the order of the semiring
\mathcal{S}
pointwise to

\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
, i.e., for
r\in\mathcal{S}
,
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
, and

\{m_{i}\}_{i\in I}\colon I\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
a family in

\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X)
indexed by
I
, we define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}r\cdot m&\triangleq&\lambda x.\,r\cdot m(x)\\
m\cdot r&\triangleq&\lambda x.\,m(x)\cdot r\\
m_{1}\mathrel{\preceq}m_{2}&\text{iff}&\forall x\colon m_{1}(x)\mathrel{\preceq}m_{2}(x)\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{0}&\triangleq&\lambda x.\,\mathbb{0}\\
m_{1}+m_{2}&\triangleq&\lambda x.\,m_{1}(x)+m_{2}(x)\\
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}m_{i}&\triangleq&\lambda x.\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}m_{i}(x)\\
\end{array}

Finally, as weightings and their associated operations are lifted from

\omega
-continuous semirings, they satisfy many expected commutativity, associativity, and distributivity properties
(see
Section

B.1
)
.

3.2.2.
The Denotational Semantics of Policies

Intuitively, a policy
p
takes as input a history
h
and produces a set of output histories
h^{\prime}
, each output being weighted by some element from
\mathcal{S}
. The notion of weightings formalizes this: the semantics
\llbracket p\rrbracket
of a policy
p
is of type

\mathsf{H}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})
, i.e., each input history
h
is mapped to a weighting
\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)
of (output) histories, and
\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})
is the weight
p
assigns to the output history
h^{\prime}
when executed on the input history
h
. The set of all histories produced by
p
on input
h
is
\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h))
, i.e., all histories to which
\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)
assigns a non-
\mathbb{0}
weight. It is useful to note that, operationally, only the
head
of the input
h
is relevant for the execution of
p
in the sense that for all packets
\pi
and all histories
h,h^{\prime}
, we have

\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\langle\rangle)(h^{\prime})~{}={}~\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}h)(h^{\prime}{::}h)~.

Predicates, modification, and duplication produce at most one output history (behaving analogously
to
NetKAT

(
Anderson et al. 2014
)
). We embed their semantics into
wNetKAT
via the unit
\eta
of

\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})
. Sequential composition, weighting, choice, and iteration yield
wNetKAT
’s
generic capabilities for modeling
quantitative
aspects of networks and require a more
involved treatment.

Predicates.
Recall that predicates
t
are Boolean combinations of

\mathsf{false}
,
\mathsf{true}
, and tests
f=n
. Intuitively, if the head packet
\pi
satisfies

t
, then executing
t
does not alter the input history—it is effectless in the sense
that it simply outputs the input history. Otherwise, i.e., if
\pi
does not satisfy
t
,
then
\pi
is dropped. Semantically, this is captured as follows:

\llbracket t\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h^{\prime})=[\text{$\pi$ satisfies $t$ and
$\pi{::}\hbar=h^{\prime}$}]
.

Modification.
The policy
f\leftarrow n
sets the field
f
of the input history’s head packet to
n
. To capture this, we use
\pi[f\coloneq n]
to denote the packet obtained from
\pi
by updating the value of the field
f
to the value
n

and define
\llbracket f\leftarrow n\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)~{}={}~\eta\left(\pi[f\coloneq n]{::}\hbar\right)
, which is
\mathbb{1}
only when
h^{\prime}=\pi[f\coloneq n]{::}\hbar
.

Duplication.

\mathsf{dup}
is intended to be a logging statement for keeping track of a packet’s trajectory through a network. Its semantics makes this explicit:
\llbracket\mathsf{dup}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)=\eta(\pi{::}\pi{::}\hbar)
.

Weighting and Choice.
All constructs considered so far produce only
\{\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1}\}
-valued weightings. Weighting and choice bring the capability of modeling
quantitative
aspects of networks to
wNetKAT
. Let us consider an example to illustrate how these constructs act in concert.

Example 0.

Let
\mathcal{S}={(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty,-\infty\},\,{\max_{\mathbb{N}}},\,{+_{\mathbb{N}}},\,{-\infty},\,0)}
be the Arctic semiring and consider the simple policies

p_{1}~{}={}~3\odot f\leftarrow 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{2}~{}={}~5\odot f\leftarrow 2~.

We have

\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h^{\prime})=3+_{\mathbb{N}}\llbracket{f\leftarrow 1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h^{\prime})
, where
\llbracket{f\leftarrow 1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h^{\prime})=0
if
h^{\prime}=\pi[f\coloneq 1]{::}\hbar
and
{-\infty}
otherwise. Hence,
\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h^{\prime})=3
when
h^{\prime}=\pi[f\coloneq 1]{::}\hbar
enabling us to model that

“Modifying the input packet by setting
f
to
1
incurs a cost (latency) of
3
”

and similarly for
p_{2}
. Let us now combine
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
via a
choice
, i.e., let
p=p_{1}\oplus p_{2}
. We have

\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)~{}={}~\textstyle\max_{\mathbb{N}}(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar),\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar))~{}={}~\lambda\pi^{\prime}{::}\hbar^{\prime}.\,\begin{cases}3&\text{if $\pi^{\prime}=\pi[f\coloneq 1]$ and $\hbar=\hbar^{\prime}$}\\
5&\text{if $\pi^{\prime}=\pi[f\coloneq 2]$ and $\hbar=\hbar^{\prime}$}\\
-\infty&\text{otherwise}~.\end{cases}

Here, because the assignments in each summand of
p
are different the result of the
semiring
addition
\max_{\mathbb{N}}(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar),\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar))
will be of the shape
\max_{\mathbb{N}}(n,-\infty)
for
n\in\{3,5\}
, producing two different output histories in the support.
Now consider a small change in
p_{2}
using
the same output history
:

p_{1}~{}={}~3\odot f\leftarrow 1\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{2}~{}={}~5\odot\hbox{\pagecolor{headergray}$f\leftarrow 1$}~.

How does
p=p_{1}\oplus p_{2}
behave now? We have
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h)=\max_{\mathbb{N}}(\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h),\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h))=\max_{\mathbb{N}}(3,5)
(or vice-versa),
which is
5
if
h=\pi[f\coloneq 1]{::}\hbar
.
i.e., the choice is resolved in a
cost maximizing manner
. This emphasizes how the semiring operations determine the interplay of weighting and choice. If, e.g., instead of the Arctic semiring, we were to choose the
Tropical
semiring
\mathcal{S}={(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},\,{\min_{\mathbb{N}}},\,{+_{\mathbb{N}}},\,{\infty},\,0)}
, the choice is resolved in a
cost minimizing manner
and
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\hbar)(h)=3
.

Sequential Composition.

The weightings produced by sequentially composing policies is naturally captured by the bind
\gg\!=
operation of the monad
\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})
of weightings. We have

\llbracket p\,;q\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})~{}={}~(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket)(h^{\prime})~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h))}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime\prime})\cdot\llbracket q\rrbracket(h^{\prime\prime})(h^{\prime})~.

This is intuitive: The weight
p\,;q
assigns to
h^{\prime}
on input
h
is obtained by summing over all
intermediate outputs

h^{\prime\prime}
that
p
produces on
h
. For each such
h^{\prime\prime}
, we multiply the weight
p
assigns to
h^{\prime\prime}
on input
h
by the weight weight
q
assigns to
h^{\prime}
on input
h^{\prime\prime}
, which captures the sequential behavior of
p\,;q
.
Note that the semantics of conjunction of two predicates coincide with their
sequencing, i.e.,
\llbracket\and{t}{u}\rrbracket_{\mathsf{Pred}}=\llbracket t\,;u\rrbracket
; we sometimes
use the two combinators interchangeably for predicates.

Iteration.
Recall that
p^{*}
is, intuitively, a countably infinite choice between terminating or keeping iterating
p
. Semantically, this behavior is captured by the countable sum

\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket(h)~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(n)}\rrbracket(h)~{}={}~\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket(h)\oplus\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\oplus\llbracket p\,;p\rrbracket(h)\oplus\ldots~.

As a sanity check for this definition, we establish the usual least fixed characterization
from
KAT
and
NetKAT

(
Anderson et al. 2014
)
, i.e.,
\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket
is the least solution of

\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket~{}={}~\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\oplus p\,;p^{*}\rrbracket

(see
Section

B.2
)
.

4.
Language Model

In this section, we define the language model of
wNetKAT
, in which each policy
p
is assigned a
weighting of guarded strings
, generalizing
NetKAT
’s languages of guarded strings.

4.1.
Reduced Syntax

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\textbf{Complete Tests}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\mathsf{Pk}^{?}\ni\pi?&\triangleq&\and{\and{f_{1}=\pi.f_{1}}{\dots}}{f_{k}=\pi.f_{k}}\\
\lx@intercol\textbf{Complete Assignments}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\mathsf{Pk}^{!}\ni\pi!&\triangleq&f_{1}\leftarrow\pi.f_{1}\,;\;\,\dots\,\,;\,f_{k}\leftarrow\pi.f_{k}\end{array}\quad\vrule\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\lx@intercol\textbf{Reduced Policies}\hfil\lx@intercol\\
\mathsf{Pol}^{\downarrow}\ni p,q&::=&\pi?\ |\ \pi!\ |\ \mathsf{dup}\ |\ p\,;q\\
&|&r\odot p\ |\ p\oplus q\ |\ p^{*}\end{array}

Figure 7
.
Reduced
wNetKAT
Syntax.

First, we restrict all
wNetKAT
policies to a reduced syntax, see
Figure

7
, without loss of expressivity. At the core of our reduced syntax are
complete tests
and
complete assignments
.
A
complete test
is a conjunction of tests
\and{f_{1}=n_{1}}{\and{\dots}{f_{k}=n_{k}}}
, covering all
f_{i}\in\mathsf{F}
. In particular, note that this conjunction over all fields means that complete tests precisely match one and only one packet:
\pi\triangleq\{f_{1}=n_{1},\dots,f_{k}=n_{k}\}
. As such the complete test matching packet
\pi
is labelled
\pi?
, and the set of all complete tests denoted
\mathsf{Pk}^{?}
. A complete test is often called an
atom
as complete tests are precisely the minimal nonzero elements of the Boolean algebra generated by basic tests
f_{i}=n_{i}
.
Dually, a
complete assignment
is an expression
\pi!\triangleq f_{1}\leftarrow n_{1}\,;\dots\,;f_{k}\leftarrow n_{k}
. We call
\mathsf{Pk}^{!}
the set of all complete assignments. It is easy to see that there are isomorphisms between
\mathsf{Pk}^{!}
,
\mathsf{Pk}
, and
\mathsf{Pk}^{?}
. Hence, we often use simply
\pi\in\mathsf{Pk}
to represent the respective complete test or assignment.

Note that every reduced policy
p\in\mathsf{Pol}^{\downarrow}
is itself a standard
wNetKAT
policy (i.e.,
\mathsf{Pol}^{\downarrow}\subset\mathsf{Pol}
). Most importantly, every policy can be converted to a semantically equivalent
reduced
policy.
Therefore, from now on, we
assume w.l.o.g. that all policies are reduced
.

4.2.
Guarded Strings: Basic Notation and Operations

Guarded strings appeared originally in the work of Kaplan
(
Kaplan 1969
)
and later played a prominent role in the work of Kozen
(
Kozen 1997
)
to reason about program (trace) equivalence. Formally, guarded strings are elements of the set
\mathsf{GS}\subset\mathsf{Pol}^{\downarrow}
, defined inductively as:

\mathsf{GS}\triangleq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{GS}^{i}\qquad\mathsf{GS}^{0}\triangleq\{\alpha?\,;\beta!\mid\alpha,\beta\in\mathsf{Pk}\}\qquad\mathsf{GS}^{i+1}\triangleq\{x\,;\mathsf{dup}\,;\gamma!\mid x\in\mathsf{GS}^{i},\gamma\in\mathsf{Pk}\}

Guarded strings encompass the minimal nonzero elements of the standard model of
NetKAT
and represent
complete packet traces
. This is analogous to the language models of KA(T) in which expressions are interpreted as regular sets of minimal nonzero (join-irreducible) terms.
For convenience, we will exploit the above mentioned isomorphisms of complete tests and assignments, and represent guarded strings as regular strings over packets (
\mathsf{GS}\cong\mathsf{Pk}\cdot(\mathsf{Pk}\cdot\mathsf{dup})^{*}\cdot\mathsf{Pk}
).
A crucial difference, however, between guarded and regular strings is that the concatenation operation captures the consistency of state between two sequentially composed programs. Given two guarded strings
\alpha x\beta,\gamma y\xi\in\mathsf{GS}
their
guarded concatenation
(see
Figure

8
(right))
\alpha x\beta\diamond\gamma y\xi
is a
partial
operation. The final state
\beta
of the first string has to be compatible with the initial state
\gamma
of the second string, that is
\beta=\gamma
for the concatenation to be defined:
\alpha x\beta\diamond\gamma y\xi=\alpha xy\xi
. Note that guarded concatenation can be lifted to a
total
operation over weightings of guarded strings (see
Figure

8
).

4.3.
Language Model for
wNetKAT

We now have all the ingredients to define the language model of
wNetKAT
as a class of functions
G\colon\mathsf{Pol}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})
. We remark that
NetKAT
models were given by regular sets of guarded strings, or equivalently,
functions
G\colon\mathsf{Pol}\rightarrow 2^{\mathsf{GS}}
so we are generalizing the Boolean semiring underlying sets to an arbitrary
\omega
-continuous semiring (note that
2^{\mathsf{GS}}\cong\mathbfcal{W}_{2}(\mathsf{GS})
).

\boldsymbol{p\in\mathsf{Pol}}

\boldsymbol{G(p)(x)\in\mathcal{S}}

\pi?

[x=\pi\,\pi]

\pi!

[\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}.\;x=\alpha\,\pi]

\mathsf{dup}

[\exists\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}.\;x=\alpha\,\alpha\,\mathsf{dup}\,\alpha]

p_{1}\oplus p_{2}

G(p_{1})(x)+G(p_{2})(x)

p_{1}\,;p_{2}

(G(p_{1})\diamond G(p_{2}))(x)

r\odot p_{1}

r\cdot G(p_{1})(x)

p_{1}^{*}

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}G({p_{1}}^{(n)})(x)

Guarded Strings

\mathsf{GS}\cong\mathsf{Pk}\cdot(\mathsf{Pk}\cdot\mathsf{dup})^{*}\cdot{\mathsf{Pk}}

Guarded Concatenation:

\diamond\colon{\mathsf{GS}}^{2}\rightharpoonup{\mathsf{GS}}

\alpha x\beta\diamond\gamma y\xi=\begin{cases}\alpha xy\xi&\beta=\gamma\\
\text{undefined}&\beta\neq\gamma\\
\end{cases}

Lifted Guarded Concatenation

\diamond\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})^{2}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})

(m_{1}\diamond m_{2})(x)\triangleq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{c}x_{i}\in\mathsf{supp}(m_{i})\\
x=x_{1}\diamond x_{2}\end{array}}\end{subarray}}m_{1}(x_{1})\cdot m_{2}(x_{2})

Figure 8
.
Language Model.

Definition 0 (Language Model).

Let
p
be a
wNetKAT
policy. We define the
weighted language

G(p)
of
p
as
the weighting
G(p)\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})
given by the table in
Figure

8
(left), where we write
G(p)(x)
for the weight attributed to
x\in\mathsf{GS}
by
p
.

{rlemma}

thm:deno-lang[Denotational–Language Correspondence]
For all
h\in\mathsf{H}
and
p\in\mathsf{Pol}
:

\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{supp}(G(p))}\end{subarray}}G(p)(x)\cdot\llbracket x\rrbracket(h)

The reader familiar with formal power series
(
Berstel and Reutenauer 2010
)
might notice the similarity between the inductive definition
G(p)
and
rational functions
. There is a crucial difference with the presence of
\diamond
, but we will show that, for
\omega
-continuous semirings, such functions can be recognized by a special finite automaton (
Section

5
). Furthermore, the language model enables reasoning about the behavior of
wNetKAT
policies on
complete traces
of the network, rather than separate input/output histories.

Example 0.

Consider the
wNetKAT
policy
(3\odot\mathsf{dup})^{*}
over the semiring of the extended naturals
\mathbb{N}^{\infty}=(\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\},+,\cdot,0,1)
.
Then over a single
\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}
,
G(p)(\alpha\,{(\alpha\,\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}\,\alpha)=3^{n}
.

Note how in
Example

2
the star allows for the unbounded sequencing of zero or more
\mathsf{dup}
s, each accruing a weight multiplier of
3
. This is because, if we intuitively unfold our example policy, we would see that the non-zero output weight of the guarded string
x=\alpha\,{(\alpha\,\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}\,\alpha
is produced entirely by the subexpression
{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}
:

\underbrace{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})^{*}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;3^{n}}}\equiv\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}\equiv\underbrace{\mathsf{skip}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;0}}\oplus\underbrace{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;0}}\oplus\underbrace{{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})}^{(2)}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;0}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\underbrace{{(3\odot\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;3^{n}}}\oplus\cdots

This is by no accident: unlike common string concatenation, one cannot construct longer guarded strings through the concatenation of
\mathsf{dup}
-free strings. For instance:
\alpha\,\alpha\diamond\alpha\,\alpha=\alpha\,\alpha
. It is the
\mathsf{dup}
construct that entirely determines the length of guarded strings, preventing their concatenation from collapsing the intermediate state. After all, the purpose of
\mathsf{dup}
from a denotational perspective is precisely to
duplicate
the current packet, freezing it and producing a longer history as its output. By attributing a weight to
\mathsf{dup}
, we are associating the weight (be it cost, latency, reliability, etc.) of taking another
hop
in our network, thus allowing us to reason about its paths. We can, however, also reason about the overall input-output behavior of a network, without restricting to specific network paths. In that case, we consider only
\mathsf{dup}
-free policies.

Example 0.

Consider the policy
((\{a\}\odot\alpha!)\oplus(\{b\}\odot\beta!))^{*}
over complete assignments
\alpha!,\beta!
, and the formal language semiring
(\mathcal{P}(\Sigma^{*}),\cup,\cdot,\emptyset,\{\varepsilon\})
, for
\Sigma=\{a,b\}
. We compute
G(p)(x)
for subexpressions
p
of our policy. We consider only
\mathsf{dup}
-free strings, as
\mathsf{dup}
-free policies always assign
\mathbb{0}
to longer strings.

\boldsymbol{x\in\mathsf{GS}}

\boldsymbol{\{a\}\odot\alpha!}

\boldsymbol{\{b\}\odot\beta!}

(\boldsymbol{\{a\}\odot\alpha!})\oplus(\boldsymbol{\{b\}\odot\beta!})

((\boldsymbol{\{a\}\odot\alpha!})\oplus(\boldsymbol{\{b\}\odot\beta!}))^{*}

\alpha\,\alpha

\{a\}

\varnothing

\{a\}

\{\varepsilon,wa\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}

\alpha\,\beta

\varnothing

\{b\}

\{b\}

\{wb\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}

\beta\,\alpha

\{a\}

\varnothing

\{a\}

\{wa\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}

\beta\,\beta

\varnothing

\{b\}

\{b\}

\{\varepsilon,wb\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}

Note that unlike our
\mathsf{dup}
example above, a
\mathsf{dup}
-free expression does not constrain at all the summands
{p}^{(n)}
that produce non-zero weights as part of the star computation. For
p\triangleq((\{a\}\odot\alpha!)\oplus(\{b\}\odot\beta!))^{*}
, and an example input
\mathsf{dup}
-free guarded string
x\triangleq\alpha\,\alpha
we have that:

\underbrace{p^{*}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;\{\varepsilon,wa\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}}}\quad\equiv\quad\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}{(p)}^{(n)}\quad\equiv\quad\underbrace{\mathsf{skip}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;\{\varepsilon\}}}\kern 5.0pt\oplus\kern 5.0pt\underbrace{p}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;\{a\}}}\kern 5.0pt\oplus\kern 5.0pt\underbrace{{p}^{(2)}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;\{aa,ba\}}}\kern 5.0pt\oplus\kern 5.0pt\cdots\kern 5.0pt\oplus\kern 5.0pt\underbrace{{p}^{(n)}}_{\mathclap{x\;\mapsto\;\{wa\mid w\in\Sigma^{n}\}}}\kern 5.0pt\oplus\kern 5.0pt\cdots

One may find it unintuitive that the same
\mathsf{dup}
-less string
x\triangleq\alpha\,\alpha
is assigned non-zero weight not only by
\mathsf{skip}
, but also by every positive n-th exponentiation of
p
. After all, the semantics of n-th iteration requires that the input guarded string be a representative trace of the “n-times sequencing” of
p
. However, note that the nuance of guarded concatenation (
Figure

8
) allows for precisely this:

\displaystyle G({p}^{(2)})(\alpha\,\alpha)~{}={}~G(p\,;p)(\alpha\,\alpha)~{}={}~\big(G(p)\diamond G(p)\big)(\alpha\,\alpha)

\displaystyle~{}={}~G(p)(\alpha\,\alpha)\cdot G(p)(\alpha\,\alpha)

\displaystyle\;+\;G(p)(\alpha\,\beta)\cdot G(p)(\beta\,\alpha)

Intuitively, this is because guarded concatenation of
\mathsf{dup}
-free strings will always collapse any intermediate state back into a minimal,
\mathsf{dup}
-free string:

\alpha\,\alpha\diamond\alpha\,\alpha~{}={}~\alpha\,\alpha~{}={}~\alpha\,\beta\diamond\beta\,\alpha.

We can extend this behavior to as many concatenations of
\mathsf{dup}
-free strings as needed to “match” a given
{p}^{(n)}
. This is crucial to model the input-output behavior of networks with cycles, where in the absence of trace information, one has no control over how many times a cycle is traversed from a given input-output packet pair.
As such, computing the exact semantics of star requires that we compute the semantics of an infinite amount of policies: namely, the n-th iterates of the policy, and then sum them. Any finite approximation will yield an incorrect result. As seen above,
G(p^{*})(\alpha\,\alpha)
is an infinite language, but all its n-th iterates produce only finite languages. We achieve the exact computation of
wNetKAT
policies through the use of an automata-based operational semantics.

5.
wNetKAT
Automata

In this section, we will present an operational semantics for
wNetKAT
. We will define a special weighted automaton model—
wNetKAT
automata—and show how to construct a finite automaton from any
wNetKAT
expression.
wNetKAT
Automata (
WNKA
) resemble classic weighted automata, albeit adjusted to the specifics of
wNetKAT
and its guarded string-based language model. While classic weighted automata would only consume a symbol at a time,
WNKA
consume packets in
linked pairs
, as processing guarded strings requires keeping a state of the “previous packet”.

Definition 0 (
wNetKAT
Automaton).

A
wNetKAT
automaton
(
WNKA
) is a 4-tuple
\mathcal{A}=(Q,\iota,\delta,\lambda)
where
Q
is a finite set of states,
\iota\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
is the initial weighting,
\delta
is a family of transition functions
\delta_{\alpha\beta}\colon Q\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
indexed by packet pairs, and
\lambda
is a family of output weightings
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
.

The above definition is similar to that of weighted automata: weightings
\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
are simply
\mathcal{S}
-valued “vectors” over
Q
(i.e., elements of the
Q
-semimodule over
\mathcal{S}
), and the transition functions
\delta_{\alpha\beta}\colon Q\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
are matrices
Q\times Q\to\mathcal{S}
. We push this analogy further and note that functions
\delta_{\alpha\beta}\colon Q\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
are isomorphic to
\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q\times Q)
, which we shall call
weighting matrices
. Given weighting matrices
m\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X\times Y)
and
m^{\prime}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Y\times Z)
we define their product
m\mathbin{\times}m^{\prime}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X\times Z)
:

m\mathbin{\times}m^{\prime}\triangleq\lambda(x,z).\;\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{(x,y)\in\mathsf{supp}(m)}\end{subarray}}m(x,y)\cdot m^{\prime}(y,z)

The initial and output weightings can be equivalently represented as matrices
\iota\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(1\times Q)
and
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q\times 1)
. With these notational conventions in hand, we can now more easily define the weighted language of guarded strings
\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})
recognized by an
WNKA

\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(\pi_{0}\,\pi_{1}\,\mathsf{dup}\,\pi_{2}\,\mathsf{dup}\,\dots\,\mathsf{dup}\,\pi_{n})\triangleq\iota\times\delta_{\pi_{0}\pi_{1}}\times\delta_{\pi_{1}\pi_{2}}\times\cdots\times\delta_{\pi_{n-2}\pi_{n-1}}\times\lambda_{\pi_{n-1}\pi_{n}}

Note that when
n=1
we obtain
\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(\pi_{0}\,\pi_{1})=\iota\times\lambda_{\pi_{0}\pi_{1}}
.

Example 0.

Consider the policy
p\triangleq(3\odot\mathsf{dup})^{*}
from
Example

2
. We define a minimal automaton
\mathcal{A}_{p}
such that
\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket=G(p)
, as below. We use single-line arrows for transitions between states and for initial weights, and double-line arrows for the output weight function on each state.

s_{0}
1
[\alpha=\beta]
3\cdot{[\alpha=\beta]}

\displaystyle\iota(s_{0})

\displaystyle\triangleq 1\quad\delta_{\alpha\beta}(s_{0})\triangleq 3\cdot{[\alpha=\beta]}

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha\beta}(s_{0})

\displaystyle\triangleq[\alpha=\beta]

Note how the automaton’s transitions are labeled by conditions on both the current packet and the previous one—hence the packet-pair semantics of our
WNKA
. We can then perform the computation of
G(p)(\alpha\,{(\alpha\,\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}\,\alpha)
as done through the automaton:

\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(\alpha\,{(\alpha\,\mathsf{dup})}^{(n)}\,\alpha)=\iota\times\delta_{\alpha\alpha}^{n}\times\lambda_{\alpha\alpha}=(1)\times(3\cdot{[\alpha=\alpha]})^{n}\times([\alpha=\alpha])=3^{n}

Although simple, the automaton of
Example

2
captures a good intuition of the behavior of
\mathsf{dup}
when iterated by star. As captured by the semantics of
WNKA
, a transition can only be taken by consuming a
\mathsf{dup}
from the input guarded string. If the
\mathsf{dup}
is furthermore part of an expression being iterated with star, then we must allow for the
unbounded
consumption of such
\mathsf{dup}
s. As our automata are finite, this is achieved by
looping
the transition. Equally, a
\mathsf{dup}
-free policy must not consume any
\mathsf{dup}
s from input guarded strings, and as such the corresponding automaton will not have any effectively traversable transitions. In practice, this means that although our automaton may still be comprised of many states with no transitions, these can be collapsed into a single state automaton, where the state output entirely captures the language for
\mathsf{dup}
-free guarded strings.

5.1.
From Expressions To Automata

Classically,
NetKAT
automata are constructed on-the-fly using
Brzozowski derivatives
. However, in the presence of weights, Brzozowski derivatives are known to generally not yield finite automata
(
Bonchi et al. 2014
)
. To avoid this, we instead describe a generalized Thompson’s construction (
Table

1
), that is guaranteed to terminate by traversing the syntax of the given expression.

Table 1
.

wNetKAT
Thompson construction. For expression
p
, we inductively build

\mathcal{A}_{p}\triangleq(S_{p},\iota_{p},\delta^{p},\lambda^{p})
.
We use
+
to denote coproducts of sets; given a coproduct
X+Y
,
f\colon X\to Z
, and

g\colon Y\to Z
the function
[f,g]\colon X+Y\to Z
is the copairing of
f
and
g
.
We denote by
\iota_{p_{1}}\boxtimes\lambda^{p_{1}}
the square matrix

\alpha\beta\mapsto\iota_{p_{1}}\times\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}}
.

\boldsymbol{p}

\boldsymbol{S_{p}}

\boldsymbol{\iota_{p}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{p})}

\boldsymbol{\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p}\colon S_{p}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{p})}

\boldsymbol{\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(S_{p})}

\pi?

\{\heartsuit\}

\eta(\heartsuit)

\mathbb{0}

\heartsuit\mapsto[\alpha=\beta=\pi]

\pi!

\{\heartsuit\}

\eta(\heartsuit)

\mathbb{0}

\heartsuit\mapsto[\beta=\pi]

\mathsf{dup}

\{\heartsuit,\clubsuit\}

\eta(\heartsuit)

s\mapsto\begin{cases}\eta(\clubsuit)&s=\heartsuit\wedge\alpha=\beta\\
\mathbb{0}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

s\mapsto\begin{cases}[\alpha=\beta]&s=\clubsuit\\
\mathbb{0}&s=\heartsuit\end{cases}

r\odot p_{1}

S_{p_{1}}

r\cdot\iota_{p_{1}}

\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}}

\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}}

p_{1}\oplus p_{2}

S_{p_{1}}+S_{p_{2}}

\left[\iota_{p_{1}},\iota_{p_{2}}\right]

\left[\left[\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}},\mathbb{0}\right],\left[\mathbb{0},\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{2}}\right]\right]

\left[\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}},\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{2}}\right]

p_{1}\,;p_{2}

S_{p_{1}}+S_{p_{2}}

[\iota_{p_{1}},\mathbb{0}]

\left[\left[\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{1}},\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}^{p_{1}}\times\iota_{p_{2}}\times\delta_{\gamma\beta}^{p_{2}}\right],\left[\mathbb{0},\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{2}}\right]\right]

\left[\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}^{p_{1}}\times\iota_{p_{2}}\times\lambda_{\gamma\beta}^{p_{2}},\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{p_{2}}\right]

p_{1}^{*}

S_{p_{1}}+\{\heartsuit\}

[\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1}]

\left[\left[\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime},\mathbb{0}\right],\left[(\lambda^{\heartsuit}\times\iota_{p_{1}}\times\lambda^{p_{1}})_{\alpha\beta},\mathbb{0}\right]\right]

\left[(\lambda^{p_{1}}\times\lambda^{\heartsuit})_{\alpha\beta},\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{\heartsuit}\right]

where

\delta^{\prime}=\delta^{p_{1}}+\lambda^{p_{1}}\times\lambda^{\heartsuit}\times\iota_{p_{1}}\times\delta^{p_{1}}

and

\lambda^{\heartsuit}={(\iota_{p_{1}}\boxtimes\lambda^{p_{1}})}^{*}

Crucially, this construction is guaranteed to produce an automaton whose language matches the language of its expression. By transitivity, our construction computes precisely the denotational semantics of the expression, by converting the input histories into a guarded string.

{rlemma}

thm:thompson-soundness[Soundness of Thompson]
Given a policy
p\in\mathsf{Pol}
:
\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket=G(p)
.

Corollary 0 (Equivalence of
wNetKAT
policies and
wNetKAT
automata).

Given a policy
p\in\mathsf{Pol}
:
2
2

2

The soundness of our syntax and semantics (i.e.,
and
and
Corollary

3
) is additionally mechanized in Lean; the mechanization can be found at
https://github.com/cornell-pl/wnetkat-lean/blob/pldi2026/WeightedNetKAT/Papers/PLDI2026.lean
.

\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi_{0}{::}\langle\rangle)(\pi_{n}{::}\dots{::}\pi_{1}{::}\langle\rangle)~{}={}~\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(\pi_{0}\,\pi_{1}\,\mathsf{dup}\,\dots\,\mathsf{dup}\,\pi_{n})~.

Like the classic Thompson construction, our construction works by combining simpler base automata to achieve more complex ones. This is achieved by composing the vector and matrix components in adequate ways. For example, for
p_{1}\oplus p_{2}
, the two automata are simply juxtaposed into one, with no interaction between the two, as depicted below.

\lxSVG@closescope {{
