The “quadrants” of the transition matrices created by copairing allow us to specify the behavior of two classes of transitions: the top left, and bottom right quadrants capture transitioning
inside
the two component automata, while the top right and bottom left qudrants capture transitioning
between
the two component automata. As expected, the construction for choice does not allow transitioning between automata, and the internal automata transitions remain the same.

If we look at sequencing, however, we see a more interesting case. When sequencing two automata, we want to preserve their independent transitions, while additionally being able to “jump” from the first automaton to the second. Traditionally, this is done by introducing
\varepsilon
-transitions, non-deterministically bridging each state of the first automaton into the start state of the second.
WNKA
s do not have
\varepsilon
-transitions and we produce these “jumps” by using the packets consumed on exiting a state, to instead transition into the second automaton. Formally we do this by: exiting the first automaton (
\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}^{p_{1}}
), entering the second automaton (
\iota^{p_{2}}
), and finally taking a first transition (
\delta_{\gamma\beta}^{p_{2}}
). This is equivalent to transitioning directly from the first automaton to the second, while accumulating the three weights, i.e.
\alpha\beta\mapsto\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\lambda_{\alpha\gamma}^{p_{1}}\times\iota^{p_{2}}\times\delta_{\gamma\beta}^{p_{2}}
(note that
\alpha\beta=\alpha\gamma\diamond\gamma\beta
).

The most interesting case for the Thompson construction, however, is the one of iteration. In the classic Thompson construction, iteration is achieved by introducing a new (accepting) start state (
\heartsuit
), and whenever a state of the automaton would be accepting, instead
\varepsilon
-transitioning to the new start state, where the string is either accepted or free to begin another iteration. Generalizing this principle requires care, due to the specific semantics of
WNKA
s. This is best observed in two classes of cases: when iterating over expressions with
\mathsf{dup}
vs.
\mathsf{dup}
-free expressions.

Firstly, as demonstrated in
Example

3
, a
\mathsf{dup}
-free policy, even if iterated, will only attribute non-zero weight to
\mathsf{dup}
-free guarded string inputs. However, these atomic guarded strings may still be iterated unboundedly, and unobservedly, due to the lack of a
\mathsf{dup}
. For example,
\alpha\xi=\alpha\beta\diamond\beta\gamma\diamond\gamma\xi
, so the weight of
\alpha\xi
must take into account its “longer”, unobserved equivalents. Processing such a string is akin to entering the start state (
\iota^{p_{1}}
) and exiting it directly (
\lambda^{p_{1}}
)—in this case three times—for each atomic component being sequenced. In general, this must be done for an unbounded number of intermediate atomic guarded strings, meaning the output of the new start state (
\lambda^{\heartsuit}
) must be the
star
of the combined
\iota^{p_{1}}\times\lambda^{p_{1}}
matrix, which naively would require computing an infinite sum.

Definition 0 (Matrix Star).

For a weighting square matrix
M\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X\times X)
, we define its star as
M^{*}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}M^{n}
, where
M^{0}_{xy}\triangleq[x=y]
and
M^{n+1}=M^{n}\times M
.

The star of a matrix is, however, a common operation in automata theory, also known as the matrix closure. In the past,
Bloom and Ésik 1993
developed an algorithm for computing the matrix star in terms of only the underlying semiring operations, together with the
semiring star
, a shorthand defined as
s^{*}\triangleq\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}s^{n}
. Although computing the star of a semiring element requires computing a countable sum, for all our documented semirings and their combinations, this operation is easily computed, usually in constant time
(
Mohri 2009
)
. The Thompson construction depicted in
Example

5
below provides a good view of the matrix star in action, as used to compute the output weights of state
\heartsuit
. For instance, as expected the policy maps the guarded string
\alpha\,\alpha
to the
infinite
regular language
\{wa\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}
, which is computed entirely by
\lambda^{\heartsuit}
, the starred output matrix.

Example 0.

For the policy
p\triangleq((\{a\}\odot\alpha!)\oplus(\{b\}\odot\beta!))^{*}
from
Example

3
, we obtain the following automaton through the Thompson construction:

s_{0}
s_{1}
\{a\}
\{b\}
\alpha\alpha,\beta\alpha\mapsto\{\varepsilon\}
\alpha\beta,\beta\beta\mapsto\{\varepsilon\}

(a)

(\{a\}\odot\alpha!)\oplus(\{b\}\odot\beta!)

s_{0}
s_{1}
\heartsuit
\{\varepsilon\}
\alpha\alpha\mapsto\{\varepsilon,wa\}
\beta\alpha\mapsto\{wa\}
\alpha\beta\mapsto\{wb\}
\beta\beta\mapsto\{\varepsilon,wb\}
\alpha\alpha\mapsto\{\varepsilon,wa\}
\beta\beta\mapsto\{\varepsilon,wb\}
\beta\alpha\mapsto\{wa\}
\alpha\beta\mapsto\{wb\}

(b)

((\{a\}\odot\alpha!)\oplus(\{b\}\odot\beta!))^{*}

Our second key consideration for the case of iteration is best seen when iterating expressions with
\mathsf{dup}
.
This is the “classical” setting, where it is possible to non-deterministically return to the
start state whenever an end state is reached, ready to process another iteration (as is the case
in
Example

2
). However,
wNetKAT
automata do not have a single start and end state. In fact,

every
state can be both initial and final, as
determined by the initial and output weights. This makes looping the automaton more delicate,
and requires an entirely matrix-based treatment, as demonstrated by component
\delta^{p}

of the star construction: To transition inside the automaton is to either take an internal transition
as normal (
\delta^{p_{1}}
), or non-deterministically (
+
) exit the state
(
\lambda^{p_{1}}
) and, via the new start state (
\lambda^{\heartsuit}
),
loop back into the automaton (
\iota^{p_{1}}
), and transition through (
\delta^{p_{1}}
).

5.2.
Computable Semantics of
wNetKAT

Finally, the effective computation of the Thompson construction allows us to compute the automaton for any given
wNetKAT
policy. Using the correctness of the semantics of the automaton (
Corollary

3
), and the fact that the automaton semantics is computed through matrix multiplication, which is itself computed in terms of semiring addition and multiplication, we obtain the following:

Theorem 6 (Computable Semantics).

Given a computable
semiring
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\preceq})
, the semantics
of every
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
policy is computable by compiling
it to its corresponding
wNetKAT
automaton.

Furthermore, given that the semantics of every
wNetKAT
policy is computable
by compiling to a
wNetKAT
automaton, we can verify the questions of
r
-safety
and
r
-reachability through decision procedures that we develop at the level of

wNetKAT
automata in the
following section.

6.
Decidability Results for
wNetKAT

In this section, we tie the technical results of the previous sections back to the
verification questions in
Section

2
. Consider again
verifying whether a network encoded in
wNetKAT
as
p
is
r
-safe or

r
-reachable. Semantically, these questions correspond to the following two
properties:

(
p
is
r
-safe)

\displaystyle\forall\pi\in\mathsf{Pk},h\in\mathsf{H}\colon\quad

\displaystyle\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\langle\rangle)(h)\mathrel{\preceq}r\ ,

(
p
is
r
-reachable)

\displaystyle\exists\pi\in\mathsf{Pk},h\in\mathsf{H}\colon\quad

\displaystyle\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\langle\rangle)(h)\mathrel{\succeq}r\ .

In words,
r
-safety says that
all
(out of possibly
infinitely many
) traces produced by the policy
p
have weight at most

r
. Dually,
r
-reachability says that there exists
some
trace with weight at least
r
.

We generalize techniques by
Almagor et al. 2022
to obtain
generic
decision procedures for
r
-safety and
r
-reachability for a broad class of semirings.
These procedures can produce
witnesses
, providing operators with diagnostic information (when
r
-safety is violated), and synthesize traces satisfying some desired lower bound on a quantity of interest. We illustrate this in
Section

7
via a case study.

6.1.
Decidability of
r
-safety in
wNetKAT

We begin by establishing the decidability of
r
-safety for
semirings that model a
worst-case
analysis.

{rtheorem}
thm:verif-safety
[Decidability of
r
-safety]
Let
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\preceq})
be a computable semiring such
that

s_{1}+s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}\quad\text{iff}\quad s_{1}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}~\text{and}~s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}~,

and let

p
be an
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
policy. Then

\text{``$p$ is $r$-safe'' is decidable}~.

Moreover, if
\mathrel{\preceq}
is total and
p
is not
r
-safe, then we can compute a witness, i.e.,
\pi\in\mathsf{Pk}
and
h\in\mathsf{H}
such that
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\langle\rangle)(h)\not\mathrel{\preceq}r
.

Before we describe the decision procedure, let us gain some intuition on the conditions imposed on
\mathcal{S}
. Computability is necessary to effectively compute the semantics of the
wNetKAT
policy. The condition on
+
expresses that
\mathcal{S}
models worst-case behavior: the semiring addition
“chooses”
a worst-case scenario so that upper-bounding
s_{1}+s_{2}
is equivalent to upper-bounding both
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
. This condition is satisfied by the Arctic, Probabilistic-union, and Why semirings (cf.
Figure

3
).

The decision procedure works as follows. First, we invoke
Corollary

3
, which gives us

\displaystyle\text{$p$ is $r$-safe}\quad\text{iff}\quad\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r~.

It follows from
\omega
-continuity of
\mathcal{S}
and the side condition on
+
that the latter is equivalent to

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r~.

We then proceed by showing that this infinite sum over all guarded strings
is computable
, which implies the claim. The key idea is to reduce the computation of this infinite sum to the computation of a matrix star (cf.
Definition

4
), which can be done via well-established algorithms
(
Bloom and Ésik 1993
)
.
All details are provided in

Section

G.2

.
In case
r
-safety is violated, we are—by the totality of
\mathrel{\preceq}
—guaranteed to find a witness by enumerating guarded strings
x\in\mathsf{GS}
in a breadth-first search manner until we find one with
\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\not\mathrel{\preceq}r
, where we use

Theorem

6
to compute the weight
\mathcal{A}_{p}
assigns to
x
. By
Corollary

3
,
x
can then be turned into an appropriate witness.

6.2.
Decidability of
r
-reachability in
wNetKAT

Next, we establish the decidability of
r
-reachability for semirings that model a
best-case
analysis.

{rtheorem}
thm:verif-reach
[Decidability of
r
-reachability for
wNetKAT
policies]
Let
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\preceq})
be a computable semiring such
that
s_{1}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
and

s_{1}+s_{2}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}\quad\text{iff}\quad s_{1}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}~\text{or}~s_{2}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}~,

and let
p
be a
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
policy. Then “
p
is
r
-reachable” is decidable. Moreover, if
p
is
r
-reachable, we can compute a witness, i.e.,
\pi\in\mathsf{Pk}
and
h\in\mathsf{H}
such that
\llbracket p\rrbracket(\pi{::}\langle\rangle)(h)\mathrel{\succeq}r
.

Let us again gain some intuition on the imposed conditions. Dually to
r
-safety, the condition on the semiring addition expresses that
\mathcal{S}
models best-case behavior: lower-bounding
s_{1}+s_{2}
is equivalent to lower-bounding one of
s_{1},s_{2}
. The condition on the semiring multiplication expresses that making traces longer can only make things worse since
s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
will always be smaller than
s_{1}
. These conditions are satisfied by the Tropical, Viterbi, and Bottleneck semirings (cf.
Figure

3
).

Our decision procedure works as follows. First, we invoke
Corollary

3
to get

\displaystyle\text{$p$ is $r$-reachable}\quad\text{iff}\quad\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succeq}r~.

We then exploit the conditions on
+
and
\cdot
to conclude that the above is equivalent to the existence of a guarded string
x\in\mathsf{GS}
corresponding to a
cycle-free run
of
\mathcal{A}_{p}
—for a notion of runs we define over the underlying graph structure of
\mathcal{A}_{p}
. There are only finitely many cycle-free runs, so it suffices to check
\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succeq}r
for only finitely many
x
to decide
r
-reachability. If we find such a guarded string
x
, we use
Corollary

3
to turn it into a witness.
All details can be found in

Section

G.4

.

7.
Case Studies

In this section, we demonstrate how our marriage of classic
NetKAT
’s modeling capabilities and weighted reasoning enables the automatic quantitative analysis of intricate network configurations.
For that, we use a topology based on Internet2’s Abilene backbone network (see
Figure

10
)
3
3

3

The TikZ code used in
Figure

10
was produced with the help of a generative AI software tool (Claude, Sonnet 4.6).
, which features
nodes
across several cities in the United States. Traffic can enter or exit the network from any node (e.g., a network packet entering at
\mathsf{BAY}
destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
), and every node is able to forward packets to nodes it is linked to (e.g.,
\mathsf{KAN}
can forward packets to
\mathsf{DEN}
,
\mathsf{HOU}
, and
\mathsf{IND}
).
We have additionally annotated the network topology in
Figure

10
with several quantities, such as the associated
failure rates
of each node or the
bandwidth
of each link between nodes (e.g., based on forwarding failure metrics or historical average bandwidths).

\mathsf{tid}

Path

1

\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{DEN},\mathsf{KAN}

2

\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{LA},\mathsf{HOU},\mathsf{KAN}

3

\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{HOU},\mathsf{ATL}

4

\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{IND},\mathsf{ATL}

5

\mathsf{ATL},\mathsf{DC},\mathsf{NYC}

Figure 10
.
Topology from Abilene network with links between nodes
weighted by
latency
/
bandwidth

and nodes by forwarding
failure rate
. Network tunnels are
mapped to their corresponding tunnel ID (
\mathsf{tid}
) in table.

Suppose that for certain source-destination pairs it is preferable to forward traffic through
tunnels

instead of with the usual forwarding behavior (e.g., based on
shortest-paths
or some other routing scheme).

Figure

10
highlights example tunnels available in the network to be used by specific nodes for
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound traffic. For example,

\mathsf{KAN}
is configured to use either of the tunnels

\mathsf{KAN}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{HOU}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{ATL}
(
\mathsf{tid}
3) or

\mathsf{KAN}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{IND}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{ATL}
(
\mathsf{tid}
4).
Both tunnels exit into
\mathsf{ATL}
, which in turn is configured to forward
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound traffic through tunnel
5
. In what follows, we first model this tunneling behavior in classic
NetKAT
and then demonstrate how
wNetKAT
enables quantitative reasoning. We model the network’s forwarding behavior using choice and
nested iteration
:

\begin{array}[]{rc}p_{\mathsf{KAN}}&\triangleq\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\\
\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4)\\
\quad\mathsf{else}\ \dots\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{(* Default behavior *)}}}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}\end{array}\begin{array}[]{rcl}p&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{ATL}\ \mathsf{then}\\
&&\quad(p_{\mathsf{ATL}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{ATL}\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}\ \mathsf{then}\\
&&\quad(p_{\mathsf{BAY}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{BAY}\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \dots\\
\mathit{abilene}&\triangleq&(p\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}\end{array}

Similarly to
Section

2.1
,
\mathit{abilene}
models the process of (i) forwarding a packet according to its current node (modeled by
p
), (ii) recording the packet’s state in the history (using
\mathsf{dup}
), and (iii) repeating this process (using iteration). Policy
p
branches on the packet’s current node and invokes the corresponding routing policy. Notice that these routing policies are
also iterated
—an idea dating back to
Gupta et al. 2014
. The reason for that becomes apparent when considering
p_{\mathsf{KAN}}
: we use the field
\mathsf{tid}
to keep track of the tunnel the packet is currently in (
\mathsf{tid}=0
meaning no tunnel).
\mathsf{KAN}
thus marks the end of tunnels
1
and
2
, and the start of tunnels
3
and
4
. In particular, if
\mathsf{KAN}
receives a packet with
\mathsf{tid}=0
and destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
, this packet may be forwarded
either
via tunnel
3

or

4
(using choice). Now, if
\mathsf{KAN}
receives a packet destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
on tunnel
1
, say, then we have to execute
p_{\mathsf{KAN}}

twice
to ensure that, subsequently, it is correctly forwarded via tunnel
3
or
4
. Iterating
p_{\mathsf{KAN}}
naturally captures the necessity of possibly having to execute the policy multiple times.

Consider
\mathsf{BAY}
, which is configured analogously to
\mathsf{KAN}
(i.e.,
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound traffic is forwarded via tunnels
1
or
2
). The full encoding of
\mathsf{BAY}
(and for all other examples throughout this section) is included in

Appendix

H

. By instantiating
wNetKAT
with the Boolean semiring we can, as with
NetKAT
,
already verify that the tunneled paths between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
are configured so that the two nodes are connected. This amounts to checking that the following policy is
1
-reachable:

(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}})\,;\mathit{abilene}\,;(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}}{\mathsf{tid}\neq 0})

7.1.
Verifying Reliability of Tunneled Paths with

Let us now illustrate how
wNetKAT
can serve as a
verification tool
for bounds on the
reliability
of a network’s configuration. Consider again network traffic entering at
\mathsf{BAY}
destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
. Beyond checking that
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
are indeed connected, a network provider may additionally wish to ensure that all tunneled paths between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
are sufficiently reliable. This corresponds to upper-bounding the
worst-case failures across all combinations of tunnels a packet might take
. We take the encoding from the previous section and
weight
each node’s (iterated) routing policy by its forwarding
failure rate
, instantiating
wNetKAT
with the Probabilistic-union semiring (cf.
Figure

3
):

\begin{array}[]{rc}p_{\mathsf{rel}}&\triangleq\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{ATL}\ \mathsf{then}\\
\quad{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.5\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{ATL}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{ATL}\\
\mathsf{else}\dots\end{array}\quad\quad\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{rel}}&\triangleq&(p_{\mathsf{rel}}\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}\end{array}

Now assume we wish to check that all tunneled paths between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
have a failure rate of at most
10\%
. We use
to decide whether the following is
0.1
-safe:

(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}})\,;\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{rel}}\,;(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}}{\mathsf{tid}\neq 0})

The decision procedure from
answers negatively and provides a witness:
\mathsf{KAN}
is configured to always forward
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound traffic through tunnel 3 (
\mathsf{KAN}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{HOU}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{ATL}
). However, when a packet at
\mathsf{KAN}
has just exited tunnel
2
(
\mathsf{BAY}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{LA}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{HOU}\shortrightarrow\mathsf{KAN}
),
\mathsf{KAN}
will unnecessarily reroute traffic through
\mathsf{HOU}
and incur more probability of failure. As a result, the tunneled path
2\shortrightarrow 3\shortrightarrow 5
between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
will have a failure rate of
10.3\%
. We remark that this is no longer a straightforward cycle detection as discussed in
Section

2
.
wNetKAT
enables the automatic identification of a specific combination of network tunnels that cause reliability issues.

A network provider can now use the generated witness to reconfigure
\mathsf{KAN}
to only forward packets destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
through tunnel
3
if they have not just exited tunnel
2
:

\begin{array}[]{rc}p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{safe}}&\triangleq\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\dots\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \and{\mathsf{tid}=2}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0}{(* Forward directly *)}}}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0\\
\mathsf{else}\dots\end{array}

In particular, packets exiting tunnel
2
which are destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
are now directly forwarded through tunnel 4. We can once again use the decision procedure from
, which this time answers positively: all tunneled paths guarantee a failure rate of at most
10\%
.

7.2.
Finding High-Bandwidth Tunneled Paths with

We now demonstrate how
wNetKAT
can aid network providers as a
design tool
when configuring the routing behavior: With
wNetKAT
, we can synthesize paths within a network satisfying specific quantitative properties; after which the network can be reconfigured appropriately.

Consider the following scenario: although there are several tunneled paths from
\mathsf{BAY}
to
\mathsf{NYC}
, we wish to refine the network to use high-bandwidth tunnels specifically for
video traffic
,
which should be delivered with at least
1000\mathsf{Mbps}
of
bandwidth
. We modify
the encoding of the network from the previous section (for which it is already guaranteed that all tunneled paths from
\mathsf{BAY}
to
\mathsf{NYC}
have a failure rate of at most
10\%
)
by weighting the
forwarding actions
in each tunnel by the corresponding link’s
bandwidth
, instantiating
wNetKAT
with the Bottleneck semiring (cf.
Figure

3
):

\begin{array}[]{rc}p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{band}}&\triangleq\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{if}\dots\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1250\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1750\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}\\
\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}\end{array}\ \begin{array}[]{rcl}p_{\mathsf{band}}&\triangleq&\dots\\
\\
\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{band}}&\triangleq&(p_{\mathsf{band}}\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}\end{array}

If it exists, we can now synthesize a tunneled path between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
with a bandwidth of at least
1000\mathsf{Mbps}
. By
, this can be decided by checking the
1000
-reachability of

(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}})\,;\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{band}}\,;(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}}{\mathsf{tid}\neq 0})~.

The decision procedure from
answers positively and provides the sought-after tunneled path:
1\shortrightarrow 3\shortrightarrow 5
has a bandwidth of
1250\mathsf{Mbps}
.
We now reconfigure the network to always choose this tunneled path for video traffic (identified via the field
\mathsf{vid}
) destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
, e.g.,

\begin{array}[]{rc}p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{vid}}&\triangleq\end{array}\begin{array}[]{l}\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\\
\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{vid}=\mathsf{TRUE}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\ \mathsf{else}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4))\\
\quad\mathsf{else}\dots\\
\mathsf{else}\dots\end{array}

In particular, video traffic (
\mathsf{vid}=\mathsf{TRUE}
) destined for
\mathsf{NYC}
is now always tunneled through tunnel
3
.
\mathsf{BAY}
would similarly be reconfigured to always tunnel
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound video traffic through tunnel
1
. The forwarding behavior for regular traffic remains unchanged from our previous example.

In summary, we first used
wNetKAT
to verify that the tunneled paths configured in the Abilene network provide a reliability of at least
90\%
; then we used
wNetKAT
to refine our network configuration to use high-bandwidth tunneled paths for video traffic. We focus on reliability and bandwidth in these examples, but our framework (and decision procedures) remains parametric on a semiring and can verify several network phenomena as shown in
Figure

3
. For example, we can similarly verify that for the final network configuration above, all
video traffic
in these tunneled paths is
additionally
delivered within
20\mathsf{ms}
. Instantiating
wNetKAT
with the Arctic semiring and weighting by
latency
, this would correspond to checking that the following policy is
20
-safe:

(\and{\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}}}{\mathsf{vid}=\mathsf{TRUE}})\,;\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{vid}}\,;(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}}{\mathsf{tid}\neq 0})

As before, this property is decidable by
; the decision procedure answers in the positive.

8.
Related Work

wNetKAT
is an extension of
NetKAT

(
Anderson et al. 2014
)
, which is itself an extension of

KAT

(
Kozen 1997
)
to reason about network behavior.
wNetKAT
is inspired in
large part by
ProbNetKAT

(
Foster et al. 2016
;
Smolka et al. 2017
)
, which in turn extends
NetKAT
to
model probabilistic network behavior.
wNetKAT
generalizes this idea to support
modeling several different quantitative behaviors based on the choice of
semiring. Nevertheless,
wNetKAT
remains a conservative extension by subsuming
both
NetKAT
and the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
. Although
wNetKAT
subsumes only a fragment of
ProbNetKAT
, an even smaller fragment (guarded and

\mathsf{dup}
-free) has previously been studied in a practical
setting
(
Smolka et al. 2019b
)
.

Singh 2021
and
Larsen et al. 2016
both introduce extensions to

NetKAT
parametric on semirings to model latency and other quantitative
properties. However, neither extension provides a sound translation to weighted
automata nor decision procedures for verifying quantitative network
properties. Both extensions are
more expressive at the syntax-level than
wNetKAT
(e.g. including quantitative
tests), our extension instead is intentionally chosen so that the syntax is
expressive enough to model interesting network behavior while still being able
to use techniques based on weighted automata.

More generally, several frameworks have been proposed for reasoning about
programming systems that are parametric on
semirings (e.g., see
(
Green et al. 2007
;
Brunel et al. 2014
;
Batz et al. 2022
)
).
In particular,

Batz et al. 2022
propose
weighted programming
as a paradigm for
specifying mathematical models beyond probability distributions.
wNetKAT
follows a similar approach (and is likewise parametric on
\omega
-continuous
semirings), though our focus is specifically on extending the power of
NetKAT
to reasoning about the quantitative behavior of networks. Many of our example
semirings, however, are based on their applications in weighted
programming.
wGKAT

(
Van Koevering et al. 2025
)
and
KAWT

(
Sedlár 2023
)
are both extensions to

KAT
that likewise follow a similar approach to the work by

Batz et al. 2022
.
Van Koevering et al. 2025
extend Guarded Kleene Algebra with Tests
(
GKAT
)
(
Smolka et al. 2019a
)
to the weighted setting and show the decidability of
equivalence for weighted automata up to bisimilarity. Unfortunately, we cannot
apply their results as
wNetKAT
features unguarded iteration; we refer the
reader to the work of
Wasserstein 2023
for the incompatiblity of
NetKAT
and
GKAT
.
Sedlár 2024
shows a completeness result for a
more general version of
KAWT
(Kleene Algebra with Weights and Tests).
wNetKAT
is most similar to
KAWT
(albeit in the setting of
NetKAT
which introduces
further subtleties as we discuss throughout the paper). However,
KAWT
does not provide a general language model and computable operational semantics, limiting this only to finite semirings.

Decision procedures for weighted automata have been studied
extensively in the literature
(
Krob 1992
;
Krob 1994
)
. Our decision procedures
for
r
-safety and
r
-reachability for
wNetKAT
automata adapt
results by
Almagor et al. 2022
for the Tropical semiring
over the natural numbers. Recent work by
Moeller et al. 2024
contributed techniques for efficient implementations of decision procedures over
NetKAT
automata
(which
wNetKAT
automata subsume).
In particular, they develop
symbolic
versions of
NetKAT
automata that do not explicitly enumerate their packet space and provide an ecosystem of supporting algorithms. Their approach is, however, highly tailored to deciding
equivalence
, a property that is (i) undecidable in general for weighted automata (ii) often too strong when it comes to verifying quantitative network properties. Applying these techniques in our setting is fundamentally different as it would require not only the development of a novel, symbolic representation of our

wNetKAT
automata but also direct symbolic decision procedures for
r
-safety and
r
-reachability rather than equivalence.

Finally, semirings have been used as
the foundation of other frameworks in the networking domain, including network
calculus
(
Le Boudec and Thiran 2001
)
and routing algebras
(
Sobrinho 2005
;
Griffin and Sobrinho 2005
)
. Network calculus is a mathematical framework designed for modeling and reasoning about
quantitative properties. It provides primitives for modeling the arrival, buffering, and departure of traffic in a deterministic queueing system and also models interactions between multiple flows. Unlike
wNetKAT
, the focus is more on pencil-and-paper proofs of performance bounds rather than automated verification of safety and reachabilty properties. Routing algebras model the behavior of distributed control-plane protocols like OSPF and BGP, whereas
wNetKAT
focuses on behavior at the data-plane level. Deepening the connections between these frameworks is an excellent direction for future work.

9.
Conclusion

We introduced
wNetKAT
, a framework for quantitative
network verification. We developed a denotational semantics of
wNetKAT
and an equivalent
language model. We then presented an operational
semantics based on
wNetKAT
automata to compute the exact semantics of
wNetKAT
.
This enabled the design of decision procedures for reasoning about
r
-safety and
r
-reachability in networks.
We then used the
framework to reason about worst-/best-case network guarantees over a range of practical network phenomena in the setting of Abilene backbone network.

As future work, we would like to implement practical versions of these decision
procedures over efficient representations of
wNetKAT
automata (e.g., as in
(
Moeller et al. 2024
;
Moeller et al. 2025
)
). Separately, network
verification with
NetKAT
requires having accurate models of such
systems; which can be tedious and error prone. This is only more true in the
weighted setting, and so we would like to explore learning for
wNetKAT
automata
(
Moeller et al. 2025
)
. Finally, we would be interested in
extensions to
wNetKAT
that make the language more expressive. In particular,
we would like to consider variants with quantitative tests
(e.g., as in
(
Singh 2021
;
Larsen et al. 2016
)
), which would allow expressing
network behavior dependent on quantities (e.g., load balancing).
These extensions would however complicate our
denotational and operational model, and importantly, would not allow us to
reduce our properties to
wNetKAT
automata. We leave them as a possible
direction for future work.

DFF project AuRoRA,

as well as a gift the VMware University Research Fund.

Appendix A
Preliminaries

In order to give an appropriate semantic treatment to
wNetKAT
, we
restrict the semirings that weights are drawn from to a specific class of
semirings:
\omega
-continuous semirings. In this section, we provide
definitions for these semirings and associated constructs.

A.1.
\omega
-cpos and
\omega
-continuous Functions

This section reviews basic concepts from domain theory that are
part of the definition of
\omega
-continuous semirings. For a more detailed
account, we refer the reader to
Abramsky and Jung 1995
.

Definition 0 (Partial Orders).

A
partial order
is a structure

\mathcal{D}~{}={}~(D,\,\sqsubseteq)~,

where
D
is a set and where
(\sqsubseteq)\subseteq D\times D
is a binary relation on
D
such that
(\sqsubseteq)
is
reflexive
,
anitsymmetric
, and
transitive
.

Now let
S\subseteq D
. We say that
d\in D
is an
upper bound
of
S
, if
d^{\prime}\sqsubseteq^{\prime}d
for all
d^{\prime}\in^{\prime}S
. We say that
d
is the
least
upper bound (or
supremum
) of
S
, if
d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}
for all upper bounds
d^{\prime}
on
S
. Antisymmetry of
(\sqsubseteq)
implies that, if the least upper bounds exists, then it is unique and we denote it by
\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}S
.

Definition 0 (
\omega
-Complete Partial Orders).

An
\omega
-cpo
is a partial order

(D,\,\sqsubseteq)

such that:

(1)

There exists a least element
\bot\in D
, i.e., for all
d\in D
, we have
\bot\sqsubseteq d
.

(2)

For every
\omega
-chain,
\mathcal{C}=\{d_{0}\sqsubseteq d_{1}\sqsubseteq\ldots\}
in
\sqsubseteq
, the supremum
\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}
of
\mathcal{C}
exists in
D
.

Definition 0 (Monotonic and Continuous Endomaps).

Let
(D,\,\sqsubseteq)
be an
\omega
-cpo and let

f\colon D\to D

be an endomap. We say that
f
is
monotonic
, if for all
d,d^{\prime}\in D
,

d\sqsubseteq d^{\prime}\quad\text{implies}\quad f(d)\sqsubseteq f(d^{\prime})~.

Moreover, we say that
f
is
\omega
-continuous
, if
f
is monotonic and preserves suprema of
\omega
-chains, i.e.,

\displaystyle\forall\omega\text{-$chains$}~\mathcal{C}~\text{in $D$}\colon\quad f\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}\right)~{}={}~\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\left\{f(d)~|~d\in\mathcal{C}\right\}~.

A.2.
Monoids

There are several definitions of (
\omega
-)continuous monoids and semirings in
the literature (see
[
Karner 1992
,
Ésik 2008
]
), ours are taken
from
Kuich 2011
.

Definition 0 (Monoids).

A
monoid
is a structure

\mathcal{M}~{}={}~(M,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{1})~,

where
M
is a set,
\cdot\colon M\times M\to M
, and
\mathbb{1}\in M
such that
multiplication

\cdot
is associative and
\mathbb{1}
is an identity w.r.t.
\cdot
.
We call
\mathcal{M}

commutative
if additionally
\forall s_{1},s_{2}\colon s_{1}\cdot s_{2}=s_{2}\cdot s_{1}
holds.
Moreover, we call
\mathcal{M}

idempotent
if
\forall s\colon s\cdot s=s
.

Definition 0 (
\omega
-Continuous Monoids).

An
\omega
-continuous monoid
is an
\omega
-cpo

(\mathcal{M},\,\mathrel{\preceq})~,

where
\mathcal{M}=(M,\,+,\,\mathbb{0})
is a commutative monoid such that

(1)

(\mathrel{\preceq})
is
positive
, i.e.,
\mathbb{0}
is the least element of
(\mathrel{\preceq})
,

(2)

+
is
monotonic
in both arguments, i.e.,
for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in M
,

\displaystyle s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}\qquad\text{implies}\qquad s_{1}+s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{1}+s_{3}

(3)

+
is
\omega
-continuous
in both
arguments, i.e.

4
4

4

Notice that monotonicty of
+
implies that the depicted suprema
are well-defined.
,

\displaystyle\forall\text{$\omega$-\text{chains}}~\mathcal{C}=\{s_{0}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{1}\mathrel{\preceq}\ldots\}\colon\forall s\in M\colon\quad s+\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\mathcal{C}=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}s+s^{\prime}

Every
\omega
-continuous monoid induces a summation operation defined on
countable
index sets as follows: Let
I
be a countable set and let

\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}\colon I\to M
be a family in

M
indexed by
I
. Now let
N=\{0,1,\ldots\}
such that

\mathsf{enum}\colon N\to I
is an
arbitrary
enumeration of
I
. One defines

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}~{}={}~\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{j=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}s_{\mathsf{enum}(j)}~|~n\in N\right\}~.

This supremum exists because every
\omega
-continuous monoid satisfies

s\mathrel{\preceq}s+s^{\prime}
for all
s,s^{\prime}\in M
. Moreover, it
can be shown that the above notion of countable summation is well-defined because the
value of the supremum is independent of the chosen enumeration
\mathsf{enum}
.

Lemma 0 (Properties of
\omega
-continuous monoids
[
Karner 1992
]
).

Let
(\mathcal{M},\mathrel{\preceq})
and let
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}

be a countable indexed family in
M
. We have:

(1)

If
I=\emptyset
, then
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}=\mathbb{0}
.

(2)

If
I=\{i_{1},i_{2}\}
, then
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}=s_{i_{1}}+s_{i_{2}}
.

(3)

If
J
is a countable set and
I_{j}

are countable for every
j\in J
such that
5
5

5

Here, and elsewhere throughout the paper,
\dot{\bigcup}

denotes
disjoint
union.

I=\dot{\bigcup}_{j\in J}I_{j}
, then

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{j\in J}\end{subarray}}\Big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I_{j}}\end{subarray}}s_{i}\Big)~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}~.

Note that
Items

1
,
2
and
3

are exactly the properties defining an
\omega
-complete monoid, therefore every

\omega
-continuous monoid is also an
\omega
-complete monoid.

A.3.
Semirings

Definition 0 (Semirings).

A
semiring
is a structure

\mathcal{S}~{}={}~(S,\,+,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{0},\,\mathbb{1})~,

where
S
is a set, with binary operations
+,\cdot\colon S\times S\to S
, and constants
\mathbb{0},\mathbb{1}\in S
such that

(1)

(S,\,+,\,\mathbb{0})
is a commutative monoid,

(2)

(S,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{1})
is a (not necessarily commutative) monoid,

(3)

multiplication

\cdot
distributes over
addition

+
, i.e., for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in S
,

s_{1}\cdot(s_{2}+s_{3})~{}={}~s_{1}\cdot s_{2}+s_{1}\cdot s_{3}\quad\text{and}\quad(s_{2}+s_{3})\cdot s_{1}~{}={}~s_{2}\cdot s_{1}+s_{3}\cdot s_{1}~,

where we assume throughout that
\cdot
binds stronger than
+
.

(4)

multiplying with
\mathbb{0}
is
annihilating
, i.e.,
\forall s\in S\colon s\cdot\mathbb{0}=\mathbb{0}\cdot s=\mathbb{0}
.

Definition 0 (
\omega
-Continuous Semirings).

An
\omega
-continuous semiring
is an
\omega
-cpo

(\mathcal{S},\,\mathrel{\preceq})~,

where
\mathcal{S}=(S,\,+,\,\cdot,\,\mathbb{0},\,\mathbb{1})
is a semiring such that

(1)

((S,\,+,\,\mathbb{0}),\mathrel{\preceq})
is an
\omega
-continuous monoid,

(2)

\cdot
is
monotonic
in both arguments,

(3)

\cdot
is
\omega
-continuous
in both
arguments.

Lemma 0 (Properties of
\omega
-continuous semirings).

Let
(\mathcal{S},\mathrel{\preceq})
and let
\{s_{i}\}_{i\in I}

be a countable indexed family in
S
.
Multiplication distributes over countable summation, i.e.,
for all
s\in S
,

s\cdot\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s\cdot s_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad\left(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}\right)\cdot s~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in I}\end{subarray}}s_{i}\cdot s~.

Note that every
\omega
-continuous semiring is also an
\omega
-complete
semiring.

Definition 0 (Computable Semirings).

An
\omega
-continuous semiring
(\mathcal{S},\,\mathrel{\preceq})
is
computable
, if:

(1)

The domain
S
is a computable set,

(2)

The operations
+,\cdot
, and the
closure operation

6
6

6

Every
\omega
-complete semiring is a so-called
starsemiring

admitting this operation
[
Droste and Kuich 2009
]
.

{(-)}^{*}\colon S\to S
defined as

{s}^{*}~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{n\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}s^{n}\qquad\text{where}\qquad s^{0}=\mathbb{1}~\text{and}~s^{n+1}=s\cdot s^{n}

are computable, and

(3)

the order
\mathrel{\preceq}
is a decidable relation.

Notation 1.

Going forward and throughout the rest of this paper, we adpot the
notational convention of writing
\mathcal{S}
instead of
S

when working over a semiring
\mathcal{S}
.

Remark 1.

Throughout the appendix, we make use in our proofs of several
properties that are well-known in the literature for (
\omega
-continuous)
monoids and semirings (e.g., countable summation can be shown to be
associative). We refer the reader to
[
Golan 1999
,
Droste and Kuich 2009
]
for
more detailed accounts.

Appendix B
Denotational semantics

B.1.
Properties of Weightings

Theorem 1 (Properties of weightings).

For reference, we list here several properties for weightings that follow
from
\mathcal{S}
being an
\omega
-continuous semiring.

(1)

(\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(X),\,+{},\,\mathbb{0}{}),\,\mathrel{\preceq})
is an

\omega
-continuous monoid.

(2)

(\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}},\eta,\gg\!=)
forms a monad,

(3)

Lifted scalar multiplication (from both sides) distributes over lifted addition,

(4)

Lifted semiring zero is an annihilator for lifted multiplication,

(5)

Lifted scalar multiplication (from both sides) distributes over countable summation,

(6)

\mathbb{0}{}
is a left-annihilator for
(\gg\!=)
,

(7)

\bot=\lambda x.\,\mathbb{0}{}
is a right-annihilator for
(\gg\!=)
,

(8)

(\gg\!=)
is monotonic in both arguments,

(9)

(\gg\!=)
is
\omega
-continuous in both arguments.

B.2.
Least Fixed Point Characterization of Iteration

We wish to show that
\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket
is the least solution of

\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket~{}={}~\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\oplus p\,;p^{*}\rrbracket~.

To formalize this, define the
\omega
-cpo
(\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H}),\,\sqsubseteq)
, where
\sqsubseteq
is obtained from lifting the order
\mathrel{\preceq}
on
\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})
pointwise. Now, given a policy
p
, define the operator

\Phi_{p}\colon(\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H}))\to(\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})),\qquad\Phi_{p}(d)~{}\triangleq{}~\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=d)~.

Theorem 2 (Least Fixed Point Characterization of Iteration).

For every policy
p
, we have

\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket~{}={}~\mathsf{lfp}~\Phi_{p}\qquad\text{where}\qquad\mathsf{lfp}~\Phi_{p}~{}={}~\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\Phi_{p}^{n}(\bot)~.

Proof.

First, we have that
\mathsf{lfp}~\Phi_{p}
is
\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\Phi_{p}^{n}(\bot)

by the Kleene fixed-point theorem (the
\omega
-continuity of of
\Phi_{p}
follows from

Theorem

1
). Then, we prove

\forall n\colon\Phi_{p}^{n+1}(\bot)=\lambda h.\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)

by induction on
n

(
Lemma

3
). The claim then follows by

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)~.

∎

Lemma 0.

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Phi_{p}^{n+1}(\bot)=\lambda h.\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

Case
n=0
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\Phi_{p}(\bot)\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\bot)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\mathbb{0}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:bot-right-annihilate-bind})}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props1})}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\llbracket{p}^{(0)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{0}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

Case
n=n+1
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\Phi_{p}^{n+2}(\bot)\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\Phi_{p}^{n+1}(\bot))&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket)}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\cdot{}\Phi_{p}^{n+1}(\bot)(h^{\prime})&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket)}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\cdot{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h^{\prime})&\text{(IH)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket)}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\cdot{}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h^{\prime})&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props5})}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket)}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\cdot{}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h^{\prime})&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props1},
assoc. of countable sum)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(k+1)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\eta(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=1}\end{subarray}}^{n+1}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(Simple arithmetic)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\llbracket{p}^{(0)}\rrbracket(h)+{}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=1}\end{subarray}}^{n+1}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\lambda h.\,\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{k=0}\end{subarray}}^{n+1}\llbracket{p}^{(k)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(Simple arithmetic)}\\
\end{array}

∎

Appendix C
Approximation of
wNetKAT
policies

\boldsymbol{p}

\boldsymbol{\left[p\right]_{n}}

p
primitive

p

p_{1}\,;p_{2}

\left[p_{1}\right]_{n}\,;\left[p_{2}\right]_{n}

r\odot q

r\odot\left[q\right]_{n}

p_{1}\oplus p_{2}

\left[p_{1}\right]_{n}\oplus\left[p_{2}\right]_{n}

p^{*}

\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}

p
primitive

::=

t

\mid

\mathsf{dup}

\mid

f\leftarrow n

\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{0}p_{i}

\triangleq

\mathsf{drop}

\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n+1}p_{i}

\triangleq

p_{0}\oplus\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}p_{i+1}

Figure 11
.
Approximants of policies in
wNetKAT
. Recall that
{{p}}^{(i)}
is defined in
Figure

6
.

We now formalize a useful notion of approximants of
\mathcal{S}
-
wNetKAT
policies
p
, which enable us to
effectively
7
7

7

Under the mild condition that the semiring
\mathcal{S}
is computable
(cf.
Definition

10
).
approximate the semantics
\llbracket p\rrbracket
of
p
with increasing precision. Besides this, approximants will enable us to prove that
wNetKAT
subsumes a rich fragment of
ProbNetKAT

[
Foster et al. 2016
,
Smolka et al. 2017
]
— a probabilistic extension of
NetKAT
.

The idea is to generalize the notion by
Smolka et al. 2017
: Determining the semantics of policies involving iteration generally requires evaluating countable sums (cf.
Figure

6
) such as

\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})~.

However, whenever we cut such a countable sum off at some index — thereby turning it into a
finite
sum — we soundly
underapproximate
it and, in the limit, recover the entire sum, i.e.,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\mathrel{\preceq}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{p}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})~.

On a syntactic level, cutting off countable sums can be realized by replacing all iterations appearing in a given policy
p
by their
n
-th unrolling, which yields the notion of
n
-th approximants:

Definition 0 (Approximants).

Let
p
be a policy and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We define the

n
-th approximant
\left[p\right]_{n}
of
p
recursively on
the structure of
p
as shown in
Figure

11
.

We often write
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}
instead of
\llbracket\left[p\right]_{n}\rrbracket(h)
. It is important to notice that
\left[p\right]_{n}
recursively unrolls
all
(including
nested
) iterations. Therefore
\left[p\right]_{n}
does no longer contain iterations so that
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)
can indeed be computed for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
by recursively applying the rules given in
Figure

6
.

Towards establishing soundness and completeness of approximants, we first observe that they give rise to an
\omega
-chain w.r.t. the order
\sqsubseteq
on
\mathsf{H}\rightarrow\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{H})
:

{rlemma}
lmm:approx-chain
The approximants of a policy
p\in\mathsf{Pol}
form an
\omega
-chain w.r.t.

(\sqsubseteq)
, i.e.

\llbracket p\rrbracket_{0}\sqsubseteq\llbracket p\rrbracket_{1}\sqsubseteq\llbracket p\rrbracket_{2}\sqsubseteq\dots

More formally, we have:

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}\sqsubseteq\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n+1}
.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by structural induction on
p
,
all cases follow from monotonicity w.r.t.
\mathrel{\preceq}
of operations
at the level of weightings:

Case
p
primitive:
We have that
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}=\llbracket p\rrbracket
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
therefore all approximants form a chain by reflexivity of
(\sqsubseteq)
.

Case
p=p\,;q
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket p\,;q\rrbracket_{n}(h)&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n+1}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-mono-l} and IH)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n+1}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n+1}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-mono-r} and IH)}\\
&=&\llbracket p\,;q\rrbracket_{n+1}(h)&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

Case
p=r\odot p
:
By
Definition

8
.
2
and IH.

Case
p=p\oplus q
:
By
Definition

5
.
2
and IH.

Case
p=p^{*}
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket_{n}\\
&=&\llbracket\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(n+1)}\rrbracket+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{($s\mathrel{\preceq}s+r$ for any $\omega$-cont. monoid)}\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n+1}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{(Simple arithmetic)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n+1}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n+1})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:omega-monoids}.\ref{def:omega-monoids2},
\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:nth-fold-mono}, and IH)}\\
&=&\llbracket\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n+1}{(\left[p\right]_{n+1})}^{(i)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket_{n+1}&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

∎

Exploiting
\omega
-continuity of the semiring operations (cf.
Figure

5
) then yields the following:

{rtheorem}
thm:sup-approx-equiv[Soundness and Completeness of Approximants]
The appoximants of a policy
p
soundly underapproximate the semantics of
p
and, in the limit, yield precisely the semantics of
p
, i.e.,

\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}\sqsubseteq\llbracket p\rrbracket\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}=\llbracket p\rrbracket~.

Proof.

By
, the supremum

\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}
is well-defined.
The rest of the proof proceeds by structural induction on
p
, all
cases follow from
\omega
-continuity w.r.t.
\mathrel{\preceq}
of operations at
the level of weightings:

Case
p
primitive:
We have that
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}=\llbracket p\rrbracket
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
therefore the supremum of all approximants is
\llbracket p\rrbracket
.

Case
p=p\,;q
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\,;q\rrbracket_{n}(h)&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{m}&\text{(\cite[citep]{[\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{domain-theory}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Proposition 2.1.12]})}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\right)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket_{m}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-omega-continuous-l})}\\
&=&\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\right)\gg\!=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket q\rrbracket_{m}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-omega-continuous-r})}\\
&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket q\rrbracket_{m}&\text{(IH for $p$)}\\
&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket q\rrbracket&\text{(IH for $q$)}\end{array}

Case
p=r\odot p
:
By
Definition

8
.
3
and IH.

Case
p=p\oplus q
:
By
Definition

5
.
3
and IH.

Case
p=p^{*}
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket(h)\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{p}^{(m)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{m\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:nth-fold-sup} and IH)}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{m}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:omega-monoids}.\ref{def:omega-monoids3})}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i=0}\end{subarray}}^{n}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(\cite[citep]{[\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{domain-theory}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Proposition 2.1.12]})}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p^{*}\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

∎

C.1.
Alternate Definiton of Approximation for While Loops

We use the following alternate definition for the
n
-th approximant of a while
loop in
wNetKAT
throughout the rest of the paper.

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left[\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\right]_{n}&\triangleq&{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(n,t)}\end{array}\quad\text{where}\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}{p}^{(0,t)}&\triangleq&\neg t\\
{p}^{(n+1,t)}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,t\,\mathsf{then}\,p\,;{p}^{(n,t)}\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{skip}\\
\end{array}

This definition will be convenient in the following section, where we relate
the semantics of
wNetKAT
to the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
. Note
that although we use an alternate definition, we are able to show that it is
equivalent to the definition given in
Figure

11
.

Lemma 0.

Let
\langle p\rangle_{n}
denote the
n
-th approximant
for a policy
p
with the alternate definition for guarded
iteration given above. This definition is equivalent to the one
in
Figure

11
:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon&\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}&=&\llbracket\langle p\rangle_{n}\rrbracket\end{array}

Proof.

By structural induction on
p
. All cases follow immediately except for
guarded iteration, as the definitions coincide exactly. We consider only
this case.

Case
p=\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p
:
We begin by unfolding the LHS/RHS:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\rrbracket_{n}&=&\llbracket\left[\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\right]_{n}\rrbracket\\
&=&\llbracket\left[(t\,;p)^{*}\,;\neg t\right]_{n}\rrbracket&\text{(Definition in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:approx})}\\
&=&\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(\left[t\,;p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(Definition in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:approx})}\\
&=&\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(Definition in \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{tab:approx})}\\
\\
\llbracket\langle\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\rangle_{n}\rrbracket&=&\llbracket{(\langle p\rangle_{n})}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(Alternate definition)}\end{array}

Finally, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&=&\llbracket{(\langle p\rangle_{n})}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:alt-defn-guarded-iteration-equiv} and IH)}\end{array}

∎

C.2.
Lemmas for Soundness and Completeness of Approximants

Lemma 0 (Monotonicity of
n
-th fold).

Given policies
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket&\Rightarrow&\llbracket{p_{1}}^{(n)}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(n)}\rrbracket\end{array}

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

Case
n=0
:

\llbracket{p_{1}}^{(0)}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket=\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(0)}\rrbracket\sqsubseteq\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(0)}\rrbracket

Case
n=n+1
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket{p_{1}}^{(n+1)}\rrbracket(h)&=&\llbracket p_{1}\,;{p_{1}}^{(n)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{p_{1}}^{(n)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{p_{1}}^{(n)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-mono-l} and assumption)}\\
&\sqsubseteq&\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(n)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-mono-r} and IH)}\\
&=&\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(n+1)}\rrbracket(h)&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

∎

Lemma 0.

Given policy
p
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\forall m\in\mathbb{N}\colon\llbracket p\rrbracket=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}&\Rightarrow&\llbracket{p}^{(m)}\rrbracket=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m)}\rrbracket\end{array}

Proof.

By induction on
m
.

Case
m=0
:

\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(0)}\rrbracket=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket=\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket=\llbracket{p}^{(0)}\rrbracket

Case
m=m+1
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m+1)}\rrbracket(h)\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n_{1}}(h)\gg\!=\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n_{2}})}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(\cite[citep]{[\@@bibref{AuthorsPhrase1Year}{domain-theory}{\@@citephrase{, }}{}, Proposition 2.1.12]})}\\
&=&\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\right)\gg\!=\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n_{2}})}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-omega-continuous-l})}\\
&=&\left(\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)\right)\gg\!=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{lmm:bind-omega-continuous-r})}\\
&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket\gg\!=\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(Assumption)}\\
&=&\llbracket p\rrbracket\gg\!=\llbracket{p}^{(m)}\rrbracket&\text{(IH)}\\
&=&\llbracket{p}^{(m+1)}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

∎

Lemma 0 (Equivalence of alternate definition of guarded iteration
for
wNetKAT
).

Given policies
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and predicate
t
in
wNetKAT
, we
have:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon&\llbracket p_{1}\rrbracket=\llbracket p_{2}\rrbracket&\Rightarrow&\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket=\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket\par\end{array}

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

Case
n=0
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{0}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&=&\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props2})}\\
&=&\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(0,t)}\rrbracket\end{array}

Case
n=n+1
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\llbracket\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n+1}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket\\
&=&\llbracket\left({(t\,;p_{1})}^{(0)}\oplus\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i+1)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket\left(\mathsf{skip}\oplus\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}t\,;p_{1}\,;{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(Definition of $n$-th fold)}\\
&=&\llbracket\left(\mathsf{skip}\oplus t\,;p_{1}\,;\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props3})}\\
&=&\llbracket\mathsf{skip}\,;\neg t\oplus t\,;p_{1}\,;\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props3})}\\
&=&\llbracket\neg t\oplus t\,;p_{1}\,;\left(\BigAdd\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}i=0\end{subarray}}^{n}{(t\,;p_{1})}^{(i)}\right)\,;\neg t\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props2})}\\
&=&\llbracket\neg t\oplus t\,;p_{1}\,;{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(IH for $n$)}\\
&=&\llbracket\neg t\oplus t\,;p_{2}\,;{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(Assumption for $p_{1}$)}\\
&=&\llbracket\neg t\,;\mathsf{skip}\oplus t\,;p_{2}\,;{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props2})}\\
&=&\llbracket t\,;p_{2}\,;{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\oplus\neg t\,;\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:weightings-props}.\ref{thm:weightings-props1})}\\
&=&\llbracket\mathsf{if}\,t\,\mathsf{then}\,p_{2}\,;{p_{2}}^{(n,t)}\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{skip}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket{p_{2}}^{(n+1,t)}\rrbracket\end{array}

∎

Appendix D
wNetKAT
Subsumes
NetKAT
and Guarded
ProbNetKAT

It is natural to ask in what sense
wNetKAT
subsumes existing (extensions of)
NetKAT
.
We establish tight connections between both
NetKAT
and its probabilistic extension
ProbNetKAT
.
Whereas it is straightforward to see that
wNetKAT
conservatively extends
NetKAT
, establishing a connection to
ProbNetKAT
is much more challenging as the semantics of
ProbNetKAT
requires an involved measure and domain-theoretic treatment.
Yet,
wNetKAT
admits an instance subsuming a rich and practically relevant
[
Smolka et al. 2019b
]
fragment of
ProbNetKAT
.

wNetKAT
Subsumes
NetKAT
.

The semantics of classical
NetKAT

[
Anderson et al. 2014
]
maps input histories to
sets
of output histories. When instantiating
wNetKAT
with the Boolean semiring
2={(\{0,1\},\,{\lor},\,{\land},\,0,\,1)}
, the semantic domains of classic
NetKAT
and
2
-
wNetKAT
hence coincide. This yields the following:

Theorem 1 (
wNetKAT
subsumes
NetKAT
).

Under the (syntactic) identifications
\and{t}{u}=t\cdot u,t\,\vee\,u=t+u
,
0\odot p=0
,
1\odot p=p
,
p\,;q=p\cdot q
, and
p\oplus q=p+q
, the syntax of
2
-
wNetKAT
reduces to classical
NetKAT
and

\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})=1\quad\text{iff}\quad\text{classical {NetKAT} produces $h^{\prime}$ when executing $p$ on $h$}~.

wNetKAT
Subsumes Guarded
ProbNetKAT
.

The semantics of
full

ProbNetKAT
maps
sets
of input histories to

possibly continuous
probability measures over
sets
of output histories,
requiring a complex measure- and domain-theoretic treatment
[
Smolka et al. 2017
]
. Loosely speaking,
this is due to the fact that
ProbNetKAT
mixes nondeterministic and probabilistic behavior,
which is well-known to be challenging. Even though
wNetKAT
does not subsume
full

ProbNetKAT
, it subsumes its rich
guarded
fragment
[
Smolka et al. 2019b
]
. In what follows, we first link
the two extensions of
NetKAT
syntactically. We then treat their semantic relationship.

Instantiate
wNetKAT
with the non-negative extended reals
(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\infty},\,+,\,\cdot,\,0,\,1)
and restrict the usage of weightings to choices of the form
r\odot p\oplus(1-r)\odot q
, which coincides with the probabilistic choice operator
p\oplus_{r}q
of
ProbNetKAT
, where
0\leq r\leq 1
. Furthermore, we restrict choice and iteration to occur guarded, in the forms
t\,;p\oplus\neg t\,;q
and
(t\,;p)^{*}\,;\neg t
. Finally, for
t,u
, we identify
ProbNetKAT
’s disjunction
t\&u
with
wNetKAT
’s
t\,\vee\,u
. Call the
the resulting instance
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
. With these syntactic identifications, the syntax of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and
guarded

ProbNetKAT
coincide.

Let us now compare the semantics of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and guarded
ProbNetKAT
. First,
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
maps input policies to discrete probability
sub
distributions over histories:

\forall h,h^{\prime}\colon\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})\in[0,1]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\underbrace{|\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)|=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h))}\end{subarray}}m(h^{\prime})}_{\text{mass of $\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)$}}\leq 1~.

The semantics of
ProbNetKAT
, denoted by
\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis
, on the other hand, maps
sets
of input histories to possibly
continuous
distributions over
sets
of histories
8
8

8

See
[
Foster et al. 2016
]
for the construction of the corresponding probability space.
, i.e., formally
\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis\colon 2^{\mathsf{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(2^{\mathsf{H}})
, where
\mathcal{M}(2^{\mathsf{H}})
is the (continuous) Giry monad
[
Giry 1982
]
. Even though
\llbracket-\rrbracket
and
\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis
are quite different in nature and
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
does not require involved measure theory,
we establish a tight connection by carefully relating the
approximants
of both semantics. First, the mass of
\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)
produced by
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
coincides with the probability of
ProbNetKAT
not producing the empty set of output histories. Second, the probability
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
assigns to
h^{\prime}
on input
h
coincides with the probability of
ProbNetKAT
producing an output set containing
h^{\prime}
.
Finally, guarded
ProbNetKAT
policies assign probability mass only to subsingleton
sets of histories (i.e., guarded
ProbNetKAT
policies have subsingleton support).

Theorem 2 (
wNetKAT
subsumes guarded
ProbNetKAT
).

Given a policy
p
in the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
and a history

h\in\mathsf{H}
, the semantics of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
subsume
the semantics of guarded
ProbNetKAT
in the following sense:

(1)

\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}[1<|a|]\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis(\{h\})=0
,

(2)

|\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)|=\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ [a\neq\varnothing]\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis(\{h\})
, and

(3)

\forall h^{\prime}\in\mathsf{supp}(\llbracket p\rrbracket(h))\colon\llbracket p\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})=\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ [h^{\prime}\in a]\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis(\{h\})
.

Proof.

We prove only (3), the reasoning for the other two claims is analogous.
Our key idea is to exploit
[
Smolka et al. 2017
, Corollary 23]
: For every Scott-continuous
9
9

9

w.r.t. the DCPO
(2^{\mathsf{H}},\,\subseteq)
of sets of histories
random variable
f\colon 2^{\mathsf{H}}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{\infty}
, we have

(
\dagger
)

\displaystyle\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ f\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis(\{h\})~{}={}~\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ f\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(h)~,

where
\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}
denotes
ProbNetKAT
’s
n
-th approximant.
This enables us to reduce the reasoning to
iteration-free
policies, and
show

(
Lemma

13
)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ [h^{\prime}\in a]\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(h)~{}={}~\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(h)(h^{\prime})~.

By ensuring that approximants of while-loops for
ProbNetKAT
stay within the guarded fragment
(
Lemma

7
), we prove

(
Lemma

5
)

\displaystyle\forall h\in\mathsf{H}\colon\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\quad\underbrace{\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)}_{\text{in {$\mathsf{Prob}$}-{wNetKAT} }}~{}={}~\underbrace{\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(\{h\})}_{\text{in}~\textsf{ProbNetKAT}}~,

where
\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}
denotes
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
’s
n
-th approximant. We
additionally show that the approximants of a policy
p
in
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT

yield precisely the semantics of
p
in the limit, i.e.,

(
)

\displaystyle\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}=\llbracket p\rrbracket~.

Finally, this implies the claim because

\llbracket{p}\rrbracket(h)(h^{\prime})~\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Thm.\ref{thm:sup-approx-equiv}}\\
{}={}\end{subarray}~\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)(h^{\prime})~\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Lmm.\ref{lmm:approx-equiv-wnketkat-probnetkat}}\\
{}={}\end{subarray}~\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\ \llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(\{h\})(\{\{h^{\prime}\}\})~\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Lmm.\ref{lmm:singleton-support-approx},\ref{eqn:thm_cantor_approx}}\\
{}={}\end{subarray}~\int_{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}}\ [h^{\prime}\in a]\ d\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis(h)~.

∎

D.1.
Equivalence Up to Approximation of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and Guarded
ProbNetKAT

In this section we derive the equivalence of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and Guarded
ProbNetKAT
up to
approximation
. We define approximation for
wNetKAT
in
Appendix

C
),
and use the following definition for
ProbNetKAT
.

Definition 0 (Semantics and approximation of
ProbNetKAT
programs).

We take the following definitions directly from
Smolka et al. 2017
:

(1)

Let

\llparenthesis-\rrparenthesis\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}(2^{\mathsf{H}})

denote the semantic map for
ProbNetKAT
, where
\mathcal{M}
is the
(continuous) probability monad

(2)

Let
\left(p\right)_{n}
denote the
n
-th approximant
of a
policy
p
in
ProbNetKAT

(3)

Let
\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(a)
denote the (discrete)
measure obtained from the
n
-th approximant:

\llparenthesis\left(p\right)_{n}\rrparenthesis(a)
.

(4)

Let
{p}^{\langle n\rangle}
denote the
n
-th fold
of a
ProbNetKAT
policy

p
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{p}^{\langle 0\rangle}&\triangleq&\mathsf{skip}\end{array}\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}{p}^{\langle n+1\rangle}&\triangleq&\mathsf{skip}\,\&\,p\,;{p}^{\langle n\rangle}\end{array}

We deviate from
Smolka et al. 2017
slightly in the definition of the
n
-th
approximant for guarded iteration (which would just be the
n
-th approximant
of its desugared policy in full
ProbNetKAT
). We use the following
alternate definition throughout:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\left(\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\right)_{n}&\triangleq&{(\left(p\right)_{n})}^{\langle n,t\rangle}\end{array}\quad\text{where}\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}{p}^{\langle 0,t\rangle}&\triangleq&\neg t\\
{p}^{\langle n+1,t\rangle}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\,t\,\mathsf{then}\,p\,;{p}^{\langle n,t\rangle}\,\mathsf{else}\,\mathsf{skip}\\
\end{array}

This alternate definition ensures that the
n
-th approximant of a policy
in the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
is also in the guarded fragment of

ProbNetKAT
(and the remainder of our proofs can proceed by induction on
approximants of guarded
ProbNetKAT
policies). Despite the deviation, we
are able to recover that this definition is equivalent
(
Lemma

7
).
With this equivalent definition of approximation in place, we show that the
guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
and
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
are equivalent
up to approximation. We define precisely what we mean by equivalent as follows.

Definition 0.

Given a weighting
m\in\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathsf{Real}}(\mathsf{H})
and a discrete
measure
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(2^{\mathsf{H}})
, we say the two are

equivalent
and write
m\sim\mu
if the following three conditions hold: (1) for all
h\in\mathsf{supp}(m)
,
m(h)=\mu(\{\{h\}\})
, (2)
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}\ |\ 1<|a|\}\end{subarray}}\mu(\{a\})=0
, and (3)
|m|=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}h\in\mathsf{H}\end{subarray}}\mu(\{\{h\}\})
.

Lemma 0 (Equivalence up to approximation of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and
ProbNetKAT
).

For all
n\in\mathbb{N}
: given a policy
p
in the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
,
the semantics of
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and the guarded fragment of
ProbNetKAT
are equivalent up to
n
-th approximation:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\forall h\in\mathsf{H}\colon&\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}(h)&\sim&\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(\{h\})\end{array}

Proof.

By
Lemma

13
, we have that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{a\in 2^{\mathsf{H}}\ |\ 1<|a|\}\end{subarray}}\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}(\{h\})(\{a\})=0
.
We prove the remaining two conditions of
Definition

4
by structural induction on
the policy
p
.

Case
p
predicate:
By
Lemma

19
.

Case
p=f\leftarrow n
:
By
Lemma

14
.

Case
p=\mathsf{dup}
:
By
Lemma

14
.

Case
p=p\,;q
:
By
Lemma

16
and IH.

Case
p=\mathsf{if}\,t\,\mathsf{then}\,p\,\mathsf{else}\,q
:
By
Lemma

20
and IH.

Case
p=\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p
:

We first unfold the LHS/RHS.

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\rrbracket_{n}&=&\llbracket\left[\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\right]_{n}\rrbracket&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:alt-approx-equiv})}\\
\\
\llparenthesis\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\rrparenthesis_{n}&=&\llparenthesis\left(\mathsf{while}\,t\,\mathsf{do}\,p\right)_{n}\rrparenthesis&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&\llparenthesis{(\left(p\right)_{n})}^{\langle n,t\rangle}\rrparenthesis&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:probnetkat-alt-approx-pol-equiv})}\end{array}

Finally, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rclrc}\llbracket{(\left[p\right]_{n})}^{(n,t)}\rrbracket&=&\llparenthesis{(\left(p\right)_{n})}^{\langle n,t\rangle}\rrparenthesis&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:guarded-iteration-equiv-wnetkat-gprobnetkat} and IH)}\end{array}

Case
p=p\oplus_{r}q
:

We first unfold the LHS/RHS.

\begin{array}[]{rclr}\llbracket p\oplus_{r}q\rrbracket_{n}&=&\llbracket r\odot p\oplus(1-r)\odot q\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\\
&=&r\cdot\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}+(1-r)\cdot\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\\
\\
\llparenthesis p\oplus_{r}q\rrparenthesis_{n}&=&r\cdot\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}+(1-r)\cdot\llparenthesis q\rrparenthesis_{n}&\text{(By definition)}\end{array}

By
Lemma

17
, we have:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}r\cdot\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}&\sim&r\cdot\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}~,&\text{(with IH for $p$)}\\
&\text{and}&\\
(1-r)\cdot\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\sim&(1-r)\cdot\llparenthesis q\rrparenthesis_{n}~.&\text{(with IH for $q$)}\end{array}

Finally, we conclude:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}r\cdot\llbracket p\rrbracket_{n}+(1-r)\cdot\llbracket q\rrbracket_{n}&\sim&r\cdot\llparenthesis p\rrparenthesis_{n}+(1-r)\cdot\llparenthesis q\rrparenthesis_{n}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lmm:addition-equiv-wnetkat-gprobnetkat})}\end{array}

∎

D.2.
Lemmas for Relation Between
\mathsf{Prob}
-
wNetKAT
and
ProbNetKAT

Lemma 0 (Equivalence of alternate definition of guarded iteration
for
ProbNetKAT
).

Given policies
p_{1}
,
p_{2}
and predicate
t
in
ProbNetKAT
, we
have:

\begin{array}[]{lrcl}\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon&\llparenthesis p_{1}\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis p_{2}\rrparenthesis&\Rightarrow&\llparenthesis{(t\,;p_{1})}^{\langle n\rangle}\,;\neg t\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis{p_{2}}^{\langle n,t\rangle}\rrparenthesis\par\end{array}

Proof.

By induction on
n
.

Case
n=0
:

\llparenthesis{(t\,;p_{1})}^{\langle 0\rangle}\,;\neg t\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\mathsf{skip}\,;\neg t\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis\neg t\rrparenthesis=\llparenthesis{(\left(p\right)_{0})}^{\langle 0,t\rangle}\rrparenthesis

Case
n=n+1
:

\begin{array}[]{rclr}&&\llparenthesis{(t\,;p_{1})}^{\langle n+1\rangle}\,;\neg t\rrparenthesis\\
