Appendix G
Decidability Results for
wNetKAT

This appendix section includes the proofs of the main decidability results in

Section

6
. We include a few additional definitions omitted in the
main body of the paper, beginning with the decision problems at the level of

wNetKAT
automata that we show to be decidable.

Definition 0 (Decision problems for
wNetKAT
automata).

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
wNetKAT
automaton over a computable semiring. We define
the following decision problems:

(1)

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
r
-safe

(denoted
\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}
) iff
\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r

(2)

We say that
\mathcal{A}
is
r
-reachable

(denoted
\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-REACHABLE}
) iff
\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succeq}r

Definition 0 (Extended family of transition functions and output weights).

Given a
wNetKAT
automaton
\mathcal{A}
:

(1)

We extend the family of transition functions
\delta_{\alpha\beta}
to
define an
extended family of transition functions

\{\delta_{x}^{*}\}_{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\colon\mathsf{GS}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q)
:

\delta_{\alpha\beta}^{*}=\mathsf{id}_{Q\times Q}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\delta_{\alpha\,;\beta\,;y}^{*}=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\times\delta_{\beta\,;y}^{*}~.

(2)

We extend the family of output weights
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}

to an
extended family of output weightings

\{\lambda_{x}^{*}\}_{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\colon\mathsf{GS}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})
:

\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{*}\triangleq\lambda_{\alpha\beta}\qquad\qquad\lambda_{\alpha\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,x}^{*}\triangleq\delta_{\alpha\beta}\times\lambda_{\beta x}^{*}~.

Notice that,
\delta^{*}
and
\lambda^{*}
are merely inductive definitions
corresponding to the weighted language of guarded strings recognized by
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. :

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x=\alpha_{0}\,;\alpha_{1}\,;\mathsf{dup}\,;\cdots\,;\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle\triangleq\iota\times\delta_{\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}}\times\cdots\times\delta_{\alpha_{n-2}\alpha_{n-1}}\times\lambda_{\alpha_{n-1}\alpha_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \iota\times\delta_{x}^{*}\times\lambda_{\alpha_{n-1}\alpha_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \iota\times\lambda_{x}^{*}

We will use the extended versions of these functions to show that we can define alternate
(but equivalent) definitions for the weighted language of guarded strings recognized by a

wNetKAT
automaton (
Sections

G.1
and
G.3
). These alternate
definitions will be useful for proving the decidability of both
r
-safety
and
r
-reachability.

G.1.
Packet Configuration Semantics of
wNetKAT
Automata

In this section, we give an alternate formulation of the weighted language of
guarded strings recognized by
wNetKAT
automaton. This alternate definition will
be useful for proving the decidability of
r
-safety. In particular, we
make the carry-on packet in the transitions of the
wNetKAT
automaton explicit by
internalizing it in the states of the automaton.

Definition 0 (Packet Configuration Semantics).

Given a
wNetKAT
automaton
\mathcal{A}
, we define its
packet configuration semantics

\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis\colon\mathsf{GS}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}
by:

\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis(\alpha_{0}\,;\alpha_{1}\,;\mathsf{dup}\,;\cdots\,;\alpha_{n})\triangleq I\times\Delta_{\alpha_{0}}\times\Delta_{\alpha_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{\alpha_{n}}\times\Lambda~,

where
I\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q^{\prime}),\Delta_{\alpha}\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}),\Lambda\colon\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q^{\prime})
for
Q^{\prime}\triangleq(Q\times\mathsf{Pk})+\{q_{\iota},q_{\lambda}\}
are defined by:

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\triangleq\eta(q_{\iota})

\displaystyle\quad\Lambda

\displaystyle\triangleq\eta(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\quad\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q^{\prime},\beta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\delta_{\alpha\beta}(q)(q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Delta_{\beta}(q_{\iota})(q,\beta)

\displaystyle\triangleq\iota(q)

\displaystyle\quad\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\triangleq\lambda_{\alpha\beta}(q)

\displaystyle\quad\Delta_{\beta}(-)(-)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathbb{0}

As for the standard definition of
wNetKAT
automata, we additionally define
an extended version of the family of alternate transition functions

\{\Delta_{x}^{*}\}_{x\in(\mathsf{Pk}\cup\{\mathsf{dup}\})^{*}}\colon(\mathsf{Pk}\cup\{\mathsf{dup}\})^{*}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(Q^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime})
:

\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}\triangleq\mathsf{id}_{Q^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}}\qquad\qquad\Delta_{\mathsf{dup}\,x}^{*}\triangleq\Delta_{x}^{*}\qquad\qquad\Delta_{\alpha x}^{*}\triangleq\Delta_{\alpha}\times\Delta_{x}^{*}~.

Again,
\Delta_{x}^{*}
is merely an inductive definition corresponding to the
packet configuration semantics of a
wNetKAT
automaton, i.e. :

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis(x=\alpha_{0}\,;\alpha_{1}\,;\mathsf{dup}\,;\cdots\,;\alpha_{n})

\displaystyle\triangleq I\times\Delta_{\alpha_{0}}\times\Delta_{\alpha_{1}}\times\cdots\times\Delta_{\alpha_{n}}\times\Lambda

\displaystyle=I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

Lemma 0 (Equivalence of packet configuration semantics of
wNetKAT
automata).

For all
x\in\mathsf{GS}
:

\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)=\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis(x)~.

Proof.

We now show by induction that for any
q\in Q
, and
\alpha x\beta\in\mathsf{GS}
,
\lambda_{\alpha x\beta}^{*}(q)=\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})
.

For the base case (
x=\varepsilon
) we have that:

(by definition of
\Delta^{*}
)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

(by definition of
\Delta
)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}(q)

(by definition of
\lambda^{*}
)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha\beta}^{*}(q)

For the inductive case (
x=\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,y
) we have that:

(by definition of
\Delta^{*}
)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,y\gamma}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{\beta}\times\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*})(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q^{\prime})\times\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}(q^{\prime})(q_{\lambda})

Now observe that although this sum is over
q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}
, only a very restrictive subset of
Q^{\prime}
gives rise to non-zero terms. In particular, by definition of
\Delta_{\beta}
,
q^{\prime}
can be at most of the form
(q^{\prime\prime},\beta)
(for the fixed
\beta
), or
q_{\lambda}
. Furhermore, by definition of
\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}
,
q^{\prime}
cannot be the state
q_{\lambda}
, as otherwise
\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}
would unavoidably multiply
\Delta_{\zeta}(q_{\lambda})(-)
, for some packet
\zeta
, which by definition would also lead to a zero term. As such this sum can be rewritten to:

(by above)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q^{\prime})\times\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}(q^{\prime})(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\beta}(q,\alpha)(q^{\prime},\beta)\times\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}(q^{\prime},\beta)(q_{\lambda})

(by definition of
\Delta
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}(q)(q^{\prime})\times\Delta_{y\gamma}^{*}(q^{\prime},\beta)(q_{\lambda})

(by the inductive hypothesis)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q}\end{subarray}}\delta_{\alpha\beta}(q)(q^{\prime})\times\lambda_{\beta y\gamma}^{*}(q^{\prime})

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=(\delta_{\alpha\beta}\times\lambda_{\beta y\gamma}^{*})(q)

(by definition of
\lambda^{*}
)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\alpha\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,y\gamma}^{*}(q)

We can then finally conclude:

(by above)

\displaystyle\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis(\alpha\,;x\,;\beta)

\displaystyle=I\times\Delta_{\alpha x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(by definition of
\Delta^{*}
)

\displaystyle=I\times\Delta_{\alpha}\times\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(associativity of matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=(I\times\Delta_{\alpha})\times\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}(I(q)\cdot\Delta_{\alpha}(q))\times\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(by definition of
I
)

\displaystyle=I(q_{\iota})\cdot\Delta_{\alpha}(q_{\iota})\times\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(multiplicative identity)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\alpha}(q_{\iota})\times\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}\times\Lambda

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}(q_{\iota})(q)\cdot\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q)\big)\times\Lambda

(by definition of
\Delta
)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}(q_{\iota})(q,\alpha)\cdot\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)\big)\times\Lambda

(by definition of
\Delta
)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)\big)\times\Lambda

(distributivity and associativity)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\big(\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)\times\Lambda\big)

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q^{\prime})\times\Lambda(q^{\prime})\big)

(by definition of
\Lambda
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\big(\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})\times\Lambda(q_{\lambda})\big)

(multiplicative identity)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\Delta_{x\beta}^{*}(q,\alpha)(q_{\lambda})

(by
\Delta^{*}/\lambda^{*}
conversion)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\lambda_{\alpha x\beta}^{*}(q)

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\iota\times\lambda_{\alpha x\beta}^{*}

(by above)

\displaystyle=\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(\alpha\,;x\,;\beta)

∎

Lemma 0.

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

Proof.

Firstly, for convenience we show that
I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda=\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})
:

(matrix multiplication associativity)

\displaystyle I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

\displaystyle=(I\times\Delta_{x}^{*})\times\Lambda

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}I(q)\cdot\Delta_{x}^{*}(q)\big)\times\Lambda

(by definition of
I
)

\displaystyle=\big(I(q_{\iota})\cdot\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})\big)\times\Lambda

(multiplicative identity)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})\times\Lambda

(applied matrix multiplication)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q\in Q^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q)\cdot\Lambda(q)

(by definition of
\Lambda
)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})\cdot\Lambda(q_{\lambda})

(multiplicative identity)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

Now we can show that:

(by above)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(
\mathsf{Pk}^{*}~{}={}~\mathsf{Pk}^{0}\,\dot{\cup}\,\mathsf{Pk}^{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}
)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{0}\,\dot{\cup}\,\mathsf{Pk}^{1}\,\dot{\cup}\,\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(disjoint union sum)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{0}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{1}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(
\mathsf{Pk}^{0}=\{\varepsilon\},\mathsf{Pk}^{1}=\mathsf{Pk}
)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(
\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=\Delta_{\alpha}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=\mathbb{0}
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{0}+\mathbb{0}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(additive identity)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

Finally, note that
\mathsf{GS}\cong\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}
with regards to
\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})
. This is witnessed by the isomorphism
\phi\colon\mathsf{GS}\rightarrow\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}
defined by:

\phi(\alpha\,x)\triangleq\alpha\cdot\phi(x)\qquad\qquad\phi(\mathsf{dup}\,x)\triangleq\phi(x)

We can then see that for all
x\in\mathsf{GS}
,
\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=\Delta_{\phi(x)}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})
. The base case is trivial:

\Delta_{\alpha\beta}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=\Delta_{\alpha\cdot\phi(\beta)}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=\Delta_{\phi(\alpha\beta)}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

For the inductive case, we have that:

(by definition of
\Delta^{*}
)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\big(\Delta_{\alpha}\times\Delta_{\beta}\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\big)(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(by definition of
\Delta^{*}
)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\alpha\beta x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

(by definition of
\phi
)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\phi(\alpha\beta\,\mathsf{dup}\,x)}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})

An equivalent argument works to show the inverse direction. As such we can form a bijection between the terms of sums over these sets, preserving
\Delta_{x}^{*}(q_{\iota})(q_{\lambda})=I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda
, and conclude:

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{>=2}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

∎

G.2.
Proof of Decidability of
r
-safety for
wNetKAT
Policies

Theorem
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

3
, we have that
p
is
r
-safe
if and only if
\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}_{p}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r
, i.e. :

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathcal{A}_{p}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}&(\text{\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:dec-problems}.\ref{def:dec-problems1}})~.\end{array}

Further, this property is decidable at the level of
wNetKAT
automata
(and we can provide a witness if it does not hold) by
Lemma

7
,
which we go over next.
∎

Lemma 0 (Decidability of
r
-safety for
wNetKAT
automata).

Given a computable
\omega
-continuous semiring
(\mathcal{S},\,\mathrel{\preceq})
,
if
for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}\in\mathcal{S}
, we have

s_{1}+s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}

iff

(s_{1}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}~\text{and}~s_{2}\mathrel{\preceq}s_{3}),

then

“Given
r\in\mathcal{S}
and
WNKA

\mathcal{A}
, does
\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}
hold?”

is decidable.
Moreover, if
\mathrel{\preceq}
is total and
\mathcal{A}\not\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}
, there is an effectively
constructible witness, i.e., some
x\in\mathsf{GS}
such that
\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r
.

Proof.

We proceed in two steps. First, we show that

\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}\qquad\text{iff}\qquad\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r~.

Second, we show that
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)
is computable, which implies the
decidability claim by decidability of
\mathrel{\preceq}
. Lastly, we will prove the claim on the
effective constructibility of witnesses.

For the first claim, consider the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-SAFE}

(
Definition

1
)

iff

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r

(
\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}
is the set of guarded strings with at most
n

\mathsf{dup}
s)

iff

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r

(assumption)

iff

\displaystyle\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r

(definition of suprema)

iff

\displaystyle\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r

(definition of countably infinite sums)

iff

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r~.

For the second claim, let
M=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}
and define the
matrix closure of
M
by

M^{*}=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}M^{i}~,

which is computable
[
Bloom and Ésik 1993
]
. We show that

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)=I\times M^{*}\times\Lambda~,

which implies the claim. To see this, consider the following:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)

(
Lemma

4
)

\displaystyle{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llparenthesis\mathcal{A}\rrparenthesis(x)

(definition)

\displaystyle{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

(
Lemma

5
)

\displaystyle{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}I\times\Delta_{x}^{*}\times\Lambda

(distributivity of
\times
)

\displaystyle{}={}~

\displaystyle I\times\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}\big)\times\Lambda

(see below)

\displaystyle{}={}~

\displaystyle I\times\big(M^{*}\big)\times\Lambda~.

For the latter equality, consider the following:

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{*}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}~{}={}~\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}M^{i}~,

where
\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}=M^{i}
follows by induction on
i
. For
i=0
, we have

\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}~{}={}~\Delta_{\varepsilon}^{*}~{}={}~\mathsf{id}_{Q^{\prime}\times Q^{\prime}}~{}={}~M^{0}~.

For the induction step, we have

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i+1}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}

(decompose words of length
i+1
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha x}^{*}

(definition)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}\times\Delta_{x}^{*}

(distributivity of
\times
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}\times\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{Pk}^{i}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{x}^{*}

(I.H.)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}\times M^{i}

(distributivity of
\times
)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle\big(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\alpha\in\mathsf{Pk}}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{\alpha}\big)\times M^{i}

(definition)

\displaystyle~{}={}~

\displaystyle M\times M^{i}~{}={}M^{i+1}~.

Let us now prove the effective costructibility of witnesses.
Assume
\neg(\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r)
. We have

\displaystyle\neg(\forall x\in\mathsf{GS}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r)

(see above )

iff

\displaystyle\neg(\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\preceq}r)

(
\mathrel{\preceq}
is total)

iff

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r

(definition of countable sums)

iff

\displaystyle\bigsqcup\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in\mathbb{N}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}{\leq n}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r

(definition of suprema)

iff

\displaystyle\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{x\in\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}}\end{subarray}}\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r

(assumption)

iff

\displaystyle\exists n\in\mathbb{N}\colon\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}^{\leq n}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r

(assumption)

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r~.

We can thus effectively construct a witness
x
with

\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r
by enumerating
all guarded strings
x
, computing
\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)
, and deciding

\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succ}r
. Since such an
x

exists, this procedure terminates.
∎

G.3.
Runs of a
wNetKAT
Automaton

In this section, we give another alternate formulation of the weighted
language of guarded strings recognized by a
wNetKAT
automaton. This
alternate definition will be useful for proving the decidability of

r
-reachability: we describe the underlying “graph” structure of
the
wNetKAT
automaton and show that reachability can be decided by considering
only finitely many paths through the graph.

Definition 0 (Runs of a
wNetKAT
automaton).

Given a
wNetKAT
automaton
\mathcal{A}
, a
run

\rho
is a string
describing a path in the automaton. We denote by
\mathsf{Runs}
the set

(Q\times(\mathsf{At}\times\mathsf{At})\times Q)^{*}\cdot((\mathsf{At}\times\mathsf{At})\times Q)

of all potential runs through the automaton.

Given states
q
and
q^{\prime}
, the runs

\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}\colon\mathsf{GS}\to 2^{\mathsf{Runs}}
from

q
to
q^{\prime}
are defined recursively as

\displaystyle\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(\alpha\,;\beta)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\{((\alpha,\beta),q)\}&\text{if $q=q^{\prime}$}\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(\alpha\,;\beta\,;y)=

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\{q^{\prime\prime}|\delta_{\alpha\beta}(q)(q^{\prime\prime})\neq\mathbb{0}\}}{(q,(\alpha,\beta),q^{\prime\prime})}\cdot{\mathsf{Runs}_{q^{\prime\prime}\to q^{\prime}}({\beta\,;y})}~.

Definition 0 (Weight of a run).

The
weight
of a given run
\rho
is defined recursively on

\rho
as:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\textnormal{weight}(((\alpha,\beta),q))&=&\mathbb{1}\\
\textnormal{weight}({(q,(\alpha,\beta),q^{\prime})}\cdot{\rho})&=&\delta_{\alpha\beta}(q)(q^{\prime})\cdot\textnormal{weight}(\rho)~,\end{array}

so that, crucially, we have for every
x\in\mathsf{GS}
:
10
10

10

Recall that
\{\delta_{x}^{*}\}\colon\mathsf{GS}\to\mathbfcal{W}_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathsf{GS})

is the extended family of transition functions defined in

Definition

2
.
1
.

\delta_{x}^{*}(q)(q^{\prime})=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(x)}\end{subarray}}\textnormal{weight}(\rho)~.

We denote by
\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}^{\cancel{\circlearrowleft}}(x)
the set of
cycle-free

runs from
q
to
q^{\prime}
on
x
. For the purposes of a
wNetKAT
automaton, a
cycle
is defined as a run where not only is a target state

q^{\prime}
repeated but the carry-on packet at the first occurrence of the target
state
q^{\prime}
in the run corresponds with the carry-on packet of the next occurrence
of the target state
q^{\prime}
in the run, i.e. a step

(q,(\alpha,\beta),q^{\prime})
in the run followed by another step

(q^{\prime\prime},(\gamma,\beta),q^{\prime})
or
((\gamma,\beta),q^{\prime})
.
Notice that, for any such run, we can consider a shorter run without this cycle
(which is relevant in the case where

\forall s,s^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}\colon s\mathrel{\succeq}s\cdot s^{\prime}
).
Additionally, the set of cycle-free runs between any two states is finite.
Finally,
\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}

(resp.
\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}^{\cancel{\circlearrowleft}}
), denotes the set of
all

(cycle-free) runs from
q
to
q^{\prime}
.

G.4.
Proof of Decidability of
r
-reachability for
wNetKAT
Policies

Theorem
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

3
,
p
is
r
-reachable if and only if

\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(p)\mathrel{\succeq}r
, i.e. :

\begin{array}[]{rl}\mathcal{A}_{p}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-REACHABLE}&\text{(\lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{def:dec-problems}.\ref{def:dec-problems2})}~.\end{array}

Further, this property is decidable at the level of
wNetKAT
automata
(and we can provide a witness if it is satisfied) by
Lemma

11
,
which we go over next.
∎

Lemma 0 (Decidability of
r
-reachability for
wNetKAT
automata).

Given a computable
\omega
-continuous semiring
(\mathcal{S},\,\mathrel{\preceq})
,
if for all
s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}
, we have (i)

s_{1}+s_{2}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}

iff
(
s_{1}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}

or

s_{2}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{3}
), and (ii)
s_{1}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
,
then

“Given
r\in\mathcal{S}
and a
WNKA

\mathcal{A}
,
does
\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-REACHABLE}
hold?”

is decidable. Moreover, if
\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-REACHABLE}
,
there is an effectively constructible witness, i.e., some

x\in\mathsf{GS}
such that

\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succeq}r
.

Proof.

We prove that
r
-reachability is decidable by showing that we
can consider only the cycle-free
runs
(see

Definition

8
) of the
wNetKAT
automaton.
We have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\in\textnormal{{$r$}-REACHABLE}

(
Definition

1
)

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\llbracket\mathcal{A}\rrbracket(x)\mathrel{\succeq}r

(By definition, (
\star
))

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\iota\times\delta_{x}^{*}\times\lambda\mathrel{\succeq}r

(
Definition

9
)

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{q,q^{\prime}\in Q}\end{subarray}}\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(x)}\end{subarray}}\iota(q)\cdot\textnormal{weight}(\rho)\cdot\lambda(q^{\prime})\mathrel{\succeq}r

(Assumption)

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\exists q,q^{\prime}\in Q\colon\exists\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(x)\colon\iota(q)\cdot\textnormal{weight}(\rho)\cdot\lambda(q^{\prime})\mathrel{\succeq}r

(Cycle-free runs suffice since
s_{1}\mathrel{\succeq}s_{1}\cdot s_{2}
)

iff

\displaystyle\exists x\in\mathsf{GS}\colon\exists q,q^{\prime}\in Q\colon\exists\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}^{\cancel{\circlearrowleft}}(x)\colon\iota(q)\cdot\textnormal{weight}(\rho)\cdot\lambda(q^{\prime})\mathrel{\succeq}r

(Every run corresponds to a guarded string, (
\star\star
))

iff

\displaystyle\exists q,q^{\prime}\in Q\colon\exists\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}^{\cancel{\circlearrowleft}}\colon\iota(q)\cdot\textnormal{weight}(\rho)\cdot\lambda(q^{\prime})\mathrel{\succeq}r

which can be decided by considering all of the (finitely many) cycle-free runs from some

q\in Q
to some
q^{\prime}\in Q
. If a corresponding run exists,
the sought-after
x\in\mathsf{GS}
can be read off that run.
Additionally, for the above proof we make the following remarks:

(\star)
: We write
\lambda
for
\lambda_{\alpha\beta}
, where the
guarded string
x\in\mathsf{GS}
ends in
\alpha\beta
.

(\star\star)
: Note that for all
\rho\in\mathsf{Runs}_{q\to q^{\prime}}(x)
,
we have that
\rho
ends in
((\alpha,\beta),q^{\prime})
, so
\lambda
remains
well-defined.
∎

Appendix H
Case Studies

We provide here the full details of the example
wNetKAT
policies given in
Section

7
for the network in
Figure

10
. We assume only the following fields:
\mathsf{node}
(packet’s current location),
\mathsf{dst}
(packet’s destination),
\mathsf{tid}
(packet’s current tunnel), and
\mathsf{vid}
(packet’s type: video or not).

H.1.
wNetKAT
Policy for Tunneled Paths

As discussed in
Section

7
, certain source-destination pairs are configured in the network to use tunnels instead of following their usual forwarding logic. We leave the default forwarding logic for each node unspecified—assuming we have, e.g.,
p_{\mathsf{ATL},\mathsf{default}}
for
\mathsf{ATL}
—and specify below only the complete
tunneling logic
of every node (as this is what is relevant for our verification questions).

p_{\mathsf{ATL}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 5\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{ATL},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=5\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{DC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{BAY}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 1\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 2)\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{DEN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{LA}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{CHI}}

\triangleq

p_{\mathsf{CHI},\mathsf{default}}

p_{\mathsf{DC}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{DC},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=5\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{NYC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{DEN}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{DEN},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{KAN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{HOU}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{HOU},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{KAN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{ATL}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{IND}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{IND},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{ATL}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{KAN}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4)\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{LA}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{LA},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{NYC}}

\triangleq

p_{\mathsf{NYC},\mathsf{default}}

p_{\mathsf{SEA}}

\triangleq

p_{\mathsf{SEA},\mathsf{default}}

All of these policies are put together to represent the entire Abilene network. We weight each (iterated) policy by its
failure rate
to check that its tunneled paths have a failure rate of at most 10%.

p_{\mathsf{rel}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{ATL}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.5\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{ATL}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{ATL}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{BAY}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{BAY}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{CHI}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{CHI}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{CHI}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{DC}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.75\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{DC}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{DC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{DEN}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{DEN}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{DEN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{HOU}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.75\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{HOU}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{IND}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.25\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{IND}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{IND}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{KAN}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.5\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{KAN}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{KAN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{LA}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}1.5\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{LA}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{LA}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}0.5\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{NYC}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{NYC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{node}=\mathsf{SEA}\ \mathsf{then}

{\color[rgb]{0.8516,0.3711,0.0078}0.75\%}\odot(p_{\mathsf{SEA}})^{*}\,;\mathsf{node}\neq\mathsf{SEA}

\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{rel}}

\triangleq

(p_{\mathsf{rel}}\,;\mathsf{dup})^{*}

We verify that all tunneled paths between
\mathsf{BAY}
and
\mathsf{NYC}
have a failure rate of at most
10\%
by checking that:

(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{BAY}}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}})\,;\mathit{abilene}_{\mathsf{rel}}\,;(\and{\mathsf{node}=\mathsf{NYC}}{\mathsf{tid}\neq 0})

is 0.1-safe. As discussed in
Section

7
, this check would fail. The complete fixed policy for
\mathsf{KAN}
so that all tunneled paths have a failure rate of at most
10\%
is as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{safe}}&\triangleq&\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\\
&&\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4)\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{default}}\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \and{\mathsf{tid}=2}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}\\
&&\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}\end{array}

H.2.
wNetKAT
Policy for High-Bandwidth Tunneled Paths

After reconfiguring the network so tunneled paths guarantee a failure rate of at most
10\%
, we show how to leverage
to find a tunneled path with a bandwidth of at least
1000\mathsf{Mbps}
. In particular, the policies in the previous section are modified so that the forwarding actions
within tunnels
are weighted by bandwidth. We show only the policies that are modified:

p_{\mathsf{ATL},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 5\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{ATL},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=5\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1750\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{DC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 1\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 2)\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1500\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{DEN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1000\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{LA}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{DC},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{DC},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=5\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1500\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{NYC}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{DEN},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{DEN},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1250\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{KAN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{HOU},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{HOU},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1250\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{KAN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1750\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{ATL}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{IND},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{IND},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}950\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{ATL}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4)\ \mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \and{\mathsf{tid}=2}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1250\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1750\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{LA},\mathsf{band}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}\ p_{\mathsf{LA},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ {\color[rgb]{0.457,0.4375,0.7031}1500\ \mathsf{Mbps}}\odot\mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

With these policies, we verify in
Section

7
that the tunneled path
1\shortrightarrow 3\shortrightarrow 5
satisfies the desired bandwidth. We then modify the
wNetKAT
encoding to model the network being reconfigured to always forward
\mathsf{NYC}
-bound video traffic through this tunneled path. The only policies that change are those for
\mathsf{KAN}
and
\mathsf{BAY}
, their complete (reconfigured) policies are as follows:

p_{\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{vid}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}

\qquad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{vid}=\mathsf{TRUE}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 1\ \mathsf{else}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 1\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 2)

\quad\mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{BAY},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{DEN}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{LA}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}

p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{vid}}

\triangleq

\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=0\ \mathsf{then}

\quad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}\ \mathsf{then}

\qquad\mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{vid}=\mathsf{TRUE}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\ \mathsf{else}\ (\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 3\oplus\mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4)

\quad\mathsf{else}\ p_{\mathsf{KAN},\mathsf{default}}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \and{\mathsf{tid}=2}{\mathsf{dst}=\mathsf{NYC}}\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 4

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=1\,\vee\,\mathsf{tid}=2\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{tid}\leftarrow 0

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=3\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{HOU}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{if}\ \mathsf{tid}=4\ \mathsf{then}\ \mathsf{node}\leftarrow\mathsf{IND}

\mathsf{else}\ \mathsf{drop}
